    \makeatletter
    \newsavebox\pandoc@box
    \newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{%
      \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
      % scaling factors for width and height
      \Gscale@div\@tempa\textheight{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
      \Gscale@div\@tempb\linewidth{\wd\pandoc@box}%
      % select the smaller of both
      \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@
        \let\@tempa\@tempb
      \fi
      % scaling accordingly (\@tempa < 1)
      \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@
        \scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
      % scaling not needed, use as it is
      \else
        \usebox{\pandoc@box}%
      \fi
    }
    \makeatother

\def\PY@reset{\let\PY@it=\relax \let\PY@bf=\relax%
    \let\PY@ul=\relax \let\PY@tc=\relax%
    \let\PY@bc=\relax \let\PY@ff=\relax}
\def\PY@tok#1{\csname PY@tok@#1\endcsname}
\def\PY@toks#1+{\ifx\relax#1\empty\else%
    \PY@tok{#1}\expandafter\PY@toks\fi}
\def\PY@do#1{\PY@bc{\PY@tc{\PY@ul{%
    \PY@it{\PY@bf{\PY@ff{#1}}}}}}}
\def\PY#1#2{\PY@reset\PY@toks#1+\relax+\PY@do{#2}}

    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim
    \makeatletter
    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.

%================ ABSTRACT =================
\chapter*{\center Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}

Machine Learning has become an essential component in modern data-driven systems, enabling automated decision-making and predictive analytics. This lab report presents a comprehensive overview of fundamental machine learning techniques implemented across seven laboratory sessions. The experiments cover key areas including data preprocessing, regression analysis, classification algorithms, encoding techniques, probabilistic modeling, and model evaluation.

Throughout the labs, various algorithms such as Linear Regression, K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), Naive Bayes, Logistic Regression, Support Vector Machine (SVM), and Decision Trees were implemented and analyzed. Additionally, techniques like label encoding, one-hot encoding, and confusion matrix evaluation were explored to enhance model performance and interpretability.

The objective of this report is to demonstrate practical understanding of machine learning concepts by applying them to real-world datasets and evaluating their effectiveness through systematic experimentation.

%================ OBJECTIVE =================
\chapter*{\center Objective}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Objective}

The primary objective of this lab report is to develop a strong practical foundation in machine learning by implementing and analyzing various algorithms and techniques. The specific goals include:

\begin{itemize}
    \item To understand and perform data preprocessing for preparing datasets.
    \item To implement Linear and Multiple Linear Regression for predictive modeling.
    \item To apply K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) for both classification and regression tasks.
    \item To learn and apply encoding techniques such as label encoding and one-hot encoding.
    \item To implement probabilistic models using the Naive Bayes algorithm.
    \item To understand classification techniques using Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machines (SVM).
    \item To implement Decision Trees and evaluate model performance using Confusion Matrix.
    \item To compare different machine learning models based on accuracy and prediction performance.
\end{itemize}


%================ INTRODUCTION =================
\chapter*{\center Introduction}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Introduction}

Machine Learning is a subfield of artificial intelligence that focuses on building systems capable of learning from data and improving their performance without explicit programming. It plays a vital role in various applications such as prediction, classification, recommendation systems, and pattern recognition.

In this lab course, several fundamental machine learning techniques were explored through practical implementation. The process began with data preprocessing, which involves cleaning, transforming, and preparing raw data for analysis. Proper preprocessing ensures that models perform efficiently and produce reliable results.

Following this, regression techniques such as Linear Regression and Multiple Linear Regression were implemented to understand relationships between variables and perform prediction tasks. Classification techniques including K-Nearest Neighbors, Logistic Regression, Support Vector Machine, and Decision Trees were studied to categorize data into distinct classes.

Additionally, encoding methods like label encoding and one-hot encoding were applied to handle categorical data effectively. The Naive Bayes algorithm introduced probabilistic classification concepts, while the confusion matrix provided a structured approach to evaluating model performance.

Overall, this report reflects a systematic progression from basic data handling to advanced machine learning model implementation and evaluation.


%================ LAB SECTIONS =================

\chapter{Data Preprocessing}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-01: Data Preprocessing}


\section*{Objective}
The objective of this experiment is to familiarize with basic data handling and preprocessing techniques required before applying Machine Learning algorithms. This lab focuses on loading a dataset, inspecting its structure, handling categorical variables, and converting them into numerical representations suitable for model training.

\section*{Dataset Description}
The dataset used in this experiment is \texttt{bank.csv}, which contains customer information related to a banking institution. The dataset includes demographic details, financial attributes, and campaign-related information. The target variable indicates whether a customer subscribed to a term deposit. The main objective is to load a CSV dataset into a Pandas DataFrame and inspect the initial records.

\section{Importing Required Libraries}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{numpy} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{np}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{pandas} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{plt}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{seaborn} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{sns}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
Machine Learning workflows rely on multiple Python libraries, each serving a specific purpose. NumPy provides efficient data structures and mathematical functions for numerical computation. Pandas enables loading, cleaning, and manipulating structured datasets. Matplotlib is used to generate basic plots and graphs, while Seaborn builds on Matplotlib to produce more informative and visually appealing statistical visualizations. Importing these libraries prepares the environment for data analysis and model development.
\subsection*{Output}
No visible output is produced if the libraries are successfully imported. If any library is missing from the environment, an error message such as \texttt{ModuleNotFoundError} is displayed.

    

\section{Loading CSV Data}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{pandas} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{pd}

\PY{n}{df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{read\PYZus{}csv}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{bank.csv}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
Pandas provides the \texttt{read\_csv()} function to load structured data into a DataFrame. Initially, the dataset was loaded without specifying the delimiter, which resulted in improper column parsing. This step helps identify formatting issues in raw data.

\subsection*{Explanation}
The dataset \texttt{bank.csv} uses a semicolon (\texttt{;}) as its field separator instead of the default comma. Therefore, the delimiter is explicitly specified while loading the file. The \texttt{read\_csv()} function stores the data in a Pandas DataFrame, which is a tabular data structure. The \texttt{head()} function is then used to display the first five records of the dataset, allowing quick verification of data correctness and column structure.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset along with all column names, confirming that the data has been loaded correctly into the DataFrame.

\section{Encoding Marital Status Attribute}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k}{def} \PY{n+nf}{replace\PYZus{}marital}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{val}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
  \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{val} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{single}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}
  \PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}

\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{marital}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{marital}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{apply}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{replace\PYZus{}marital}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the categorical attribute \texttt{marital} is converted into a numerical format to make it suitable for Machine Learning algorithms. A user-defined function named \texttt{replace\_marital} is created, which assigns the value 0 to customers with marital status \texttt{single} and the value 1 to all other categories. The \texttt{apply()} function is then used to apply this transformation to the entire \texttt{marital} column of the dataset. This process simplifies categorical data while preserving relevant information.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset where the \texttt{marital} column is successfully transformed into numerical values. Customers with marital status \texttt{single} are represented by 0, while all other marital statuses are represented by 1.

\section{Encoding Housing Loan Attribute}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{housing}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{housing}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{map}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{yes}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{no}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the categorical attribute \texttt{housing} is converted into a numerical format using the \texttt{map()} function. The values \texttt{yes} and \texttt{no} are mapped to 1 and 0 respectively. This conversion is necessary because Machine Learning algorithms require numerical input features. The \texttt{get} method ensures safe mapping of values within the column.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset where the \texttt{housing} column has been successfully transformed into numerical values. A value of 1 indicates the presence of a housing loan, while 0 indicates the absence of a housing loan.

\section{Encoding Loan Attribute}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{loan}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{loan}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{yes}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{no}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the categorical attribute \texttt{loan} is converted into numerical form to ensure compatibility with Machine Learning algorithms. The \texttt{replace()} function is used to map the value \texttt{yes} to 1 and \texttt{no} to 0. This binary encoding simplifies the data representation while retaining the original meaning of the attribute.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset, where the \texttt{loan} column is successfully transformed into numerical values. A value of 1 indicates the presence of a personal loan, while 0 indicates the absence of a loan.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{job}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{job}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{replace}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{unemployed}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{services}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{management}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{blue\PYZhy{}collar}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{self\PYZhy{}employed}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{technician}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{entrepreneur}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{admin.}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{student}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{housemaid}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{retired}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
                   \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{unknown}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The categorical attribute \texttt{job} is transformed into a numerical format to make it suitable for Machine Learning algorithms. The \texttt{replace()} function is used to map specific job categories to binary values: jobs considered as professional or managerial (e.g., \texttt{management}, \texttt{technician}, \texttt{entrepreneur}, \texttt{student}) are assigned 1, while other jobs (e.g., \texttt{unemployed}, \texttt{services}, \texttt{blue-collar}, \texttt{self-employed}, \texttt{admin.}, \texttt{housemaid}, \texttt{retired}) are assigned 0. Any unknown job entries are replaced with \texttt{NaN}. This encoding simplifies categorical data for model training.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset with the \texttt{job} column converted to numerical values. Job categories are represented as 0 or 1, and any unknown values are represented as \texttt{NaN}.

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{month} column contains the names of months as categorical string values. To convert this categorical data into a numerical format suitable for Machine Learning models, a mapping is applied where each month name is replaced with its corresponding integer value (e.g., \texttt{'jan'} = 1, \texttt{'feb'} = 2, ..., \texttt{'dec'} = 12). The \texttt{map()} function is used to transform the entire \texttt{month} column according to this mapping. This preprocessing step ensures that the month attribute can be interpreted quantitatively by algorithms.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first ten rows of the dataset after transformation. The \texttt{month} column now contains integer values representing the months, while all other columns remain unchanged.

\section{Encoding Education Attribute}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{education} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{education}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{map}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{primary}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} 
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{secondary}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{tertiary}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{unknown}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{)}

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{education} column contains categorical values representing the education level of customers. To prepare the data for Machine Learning models, these categorical values are converted to numerical values using the \texttt{map()} function. The mapping is as follows: \texttt{'primary'} = 1, \texttt{'secondary'} = 2, \texttt{'tertiary'} = 3, and \texttt{'unknown'} is replaced with \texttt{NaN} to indicate missing data. This transformation allows algorithms to process the education levels quantitatively.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first ten rows of the dataset after the transformation. The \texttt{education} column now contains numerical values (1, 2, 3) corresponding to education levels, with \texttt{NaN} for unknown entries.

\section{Unique Values of the Outcome of Previous Marketing Campaign (\texttt{poutcome})}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{unique()} function is used to identify all distinct values present in the \texttt{poutcome} column of the dataset. This column represents the result of the previous marketing campaign for each customer. Determining unique values helps in understanding the different categories present and assists in further preprocessing or encoding steps required for Machine Learning.

\section{Encoding Poutcome Attribute}        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{15}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{poutcome} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{poutcome}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{map}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{unknown}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{nan}\PY{p}{,} 
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{failure}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{other}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{success}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{poutcome} column represents the outcome of the previous marketing campaign and is a categorical variable. To make it compatible with Machine Learning algorithms, it is mapped to numerical values using the \texttt{map()} function. The mapping assigns \texttt{failure} to 1, \texttt{other} to 2, \texttt{success} to 3, and replaces \texttt{unknown} values with \texttt{NaN} to handle missing information. This encoding simplifies the dataset while preserving meaningful distinctions between campaign outcomes.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first ten rows of the dataset with the \texttt{poutcome} column transformed into numerical values. Entries that were \texttt{unknown} are now shown as \texttt{NaN}, while other outcomes are represented by 1, 2, or 3 accordingly.

\section{Normalizing the Balance Attribute}        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{16}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{apply}\PY{p}{(}\PY{k}{lambda} \PY{n}{v}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{v} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{/}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{max}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balance}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{balance} column is a numerical feature representing the account balance of customers. To scale this feature between 0 and 1, min-max normalization is applied. The formula used is:
\[
\text{normalized\_value} = \frac{v - \text{min(balance)}}{\text{max(balance)} - \text{min(balance)}}
\]
where \(v\) is the original balance value. This transformation ensures that all values of \texttt{balance} lie within the range [0, 1], improving the performance and convergence of many Machine Learning algorithms.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first ten rows of the dataset after normalization. The \texttt{balance} column values are now scaled between 0 and 1 while all other columns remain unchanged.

\section{Normalization of pdays Attribute}        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{17}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{apply}\PY{p}{(}\PY{k}{lambda} \PY{n}{v}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{v} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{/}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{max}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    
\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{pdays} column, which represents the number of days since a client was last contacted, is normalized using the Min-Max scaling technique. This transformation scales all values to a range between 0 and 1, which helps improve the performance and convergence of Machine Learning algorithms.

\subsection*{Output}
The first ten rows of the dataset are displayed. The \texttt{pdays} column now contains values scaled between 0 and 1, while the other columns remain unchanged.

\section{Feature Scaling using Min-Max Scaler}
 
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{18}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{preprocessing} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{MinMaxScaler}
\PY{n}{scaler} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{MinMaxScaler}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{duration}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{duration}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pdays}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
Feature scaling is performed to normalize the numerical attributes \texttt{duration} and \texttt{pdays} into a fixed range, usually between 0 and 1, which helps in faster convergence and better performance of Machine Learning algorithms. The \texttt{MinMaxScaler} from \texttt{sklearn.preprocessing} is used. The \texttt{fit\_transform()} method calculates the minimum and maximum values of each feature and scales all values accordingly. This ensures that the features contribute proportionally to the model training.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the dataset where the \texttt{duration} and \texttt{pdays} columns have been scaled to values between 0 and 1, while all other columns remain unchanged.

\chapter{Linear Regression}

\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-02: Linear Regression}

\section*{Objective}

The objective of this Machine Learning lab is to understand and implement regression techniques using real-world datasets. This lab focuses on applying Simple Linear Regression and Multiple Linear Regression to analyze the relationship between independent variables and a target variable. The experiments aim to develop predictive models, interpret feature influence, and evaluate model performance using appropriate regression metrics.

\section*{Dataset Description}

Three different datasets are used in this lab to demonstrate the application of regression algorithms.

\subsection*{Dhaka Home Prices Dataset}
The \texttt{dhaka homeprices.csv} dataset is used for implementing Simple Linear Regression. This dataset contains housing-related information from Dhaka city, where a single independent feature is used to predict house prices. The dataset helps illustrate how a dependent variable varies linearly with one explanatory variable.

\subsection*{Insurance Dataset}
The \texttt{insurance.csv} dataset is used for implementing Multiple Linear Regression. It contains multiple attributes related to insurance policyholders. The dataset is used to analyze how multiple independent variables collectively influence insurance-related outcomes.

\subsection*{Car Data Dataset}
The \texttt{cardata.csv} dataset is also used for Multiple Linear Regression. It includes attributes such as \texttt{speed}, \texttt{car\_age}, \texttt{experience}, and \texttt{risk}. These features are used to predict a target variable by learning the combined effect of multiple factors on the outcome.
   
    

    
\section{Linear Regression Implementation}
\label{linear-regression-implementation}

\subsection{Splitting Dataset into Training and Testing Sets}
    
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{x} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{area}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{price}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{xtest}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytest} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{x}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.40}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state} \PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}xtest}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}xtrain}
\end{Verbatim}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{xtest}


\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}xtrain}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the independent variable \texttt{area} is assigned to \texttt{x}, and the dependent variable \texttt{price} is assigned to \texttt{y}. The dataset is then divided into training and testing subsets using the \texttt{train\_test\_split()} function. Forty percent of the data is reserved for testing, while the remaining sixty percent is used for training the model. The \texttt{random\_state} parameter ensures reproducibility of the split. Displaying \texttt{xtest} allows verification of the test data samples.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the feature values of the testing dataset (\texttt{xtest}), which contains 40\% of the total data selected randomly from the original dataset.

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the Linear Regression model is imported from the \texttt{sklearn.linear\_model} module and initialized. The \texttt{fit()} method is used to train the model using the training data (\texttt{xtrain} and \texttt{ytrain}). After training, the model performance is evaluated using the \texttt{score()} method on the testing dataset (\texttt{xtest} and \texttt{ytest}). The \texttt{score()} function returns the coefficient of determination (R-squared value), which indicates how well the model explains the variance in the target variable.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the R-squared score of the Linear Regression model. A value closer to 1 indicates better predictive performance, while a lower value indicates weaker model accuracy.

\subsection{Prediction Using Trained Linear Regression Model}

\subsection*{Explanation}
After training the Linear Regression model, the \texttt{predict()} method is used to estimate the target variable for new input values. In this step, the model predicts the output for given feature values \texttt{3300}, \texttt{3200}, and \texttt{2850}. These values represent unseen data points, and the trained model applies the learned linear relationship to generate corresponding predictions.

\subsection*{Output}
The output consists of three numerical predicted values returned by the regression model. Each value represents the estimated target variable corresponding to the input values \texttt{3300}, \texttt{3200}, and \texttt{2850}, respectively.

\subsection{Handling Missing Values in Experience Attribute}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the \texttt{experience} attribute is analyzed and preprocessed to handle missing values. Initially, the \texttt{experience} column is inspected, and its mean and median values are computed to understand the data distribution. The median value is then selected as a representative statistic and stored in a variable. This median value is used to replace missing values in the \texttt{experience} column using the \texttt{fillna()} function. Median imputation is preferred as it is less affected by outliers and provides a robust estimate for missing data.

\subsection*{Code \& Output}
The output displays the \texttt{experience} column before and after preprocessing. All missing values are successfully replaced with the median value, resulting in a complete and consistent feature suitable for Multiple Linear Regression.

\subsection{Multiple Linear Regression Model Training}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, a Multiple Linear Regression model is created using the \texttt{LinearRegression} class from \texttt{sklearn.linear\_model}. Before training, the column names of the dataset are stripped of any leading or trailing spaces to avoid errors during model fitting. The model is then trained using three independent features: \texttt{speed}, \texttt{car\_age}, and \texttt{experience}, to predict the dependent variable \texttt{risk}. The \texttt{fit()} method estimates the coefficients and intercept of the linear equation that best fits the training data.

\subsection*{Code \& Output}
The output displays the column names of the dataset to verify that there are no unwanted spaces. The model is successfully fitted to the dataset, ready for prediction. No immediate numerical output is shown, but the model's coefficients and intercept can be accessed using \texttt{reg.coef\_} and \texttt{reg.intercept\_}.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{predict()} method of the trained Multiple Linear Regression model is used to estimate the target value for a new observation. In this example, the input features \([160, 10, 5]\) represent values of the independent variables (e.g., \texttt{speed}, \texttt{car\_age}, \texttt{experience}). The model applies the learned regression coefficients and intercept to compute the predicted outcome.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a single numerical value corresponding to the predicted target variable for the given input features. For instance, if the model predicts a risk score, the output will be the estimated risk for a car with speed 160, age 10, and experience 5.

\subsection{Regression Coefficients}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{36}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{reg}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{coef\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The attribute \texttt{reg.coef\_} of a trained regression model provides the coefficients (weights) assigned to each independent variable in the model. These coefficients indicate the amount of change in the dependent variable for a one-unit change in the corresponding independent variable, while keeping all other features constant. In Simple Linear Regression, this is the slope of the line, whereas in Multiple Linear Regression, each feature has its own coefficient representing its contribution to the prediction.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is an array of numerical values representing the coefficients of the independent variables. For example, in a dataset with three features, the output could look like \texttt{[0.85, -0.12, 2.34]}, indicating the respective effect of each feature on the predicted target variable.

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{36}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
array([ 0.33059217,  1.61053246, -6.20772074])
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection{Model Intercept}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{37}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{reg}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{intercept\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{reg.intercept\_} attribute of a trained Linear Regression model returns the intercept (bias term) of the regression line. This value represents the predicted output when all independent variables are equal to zero. It is an essential part of the regression equation: 

\[
y = b_0 + b_1x_1 + b_2x_2 + \dots + b_nx_n
\]

where \(b_0\) is the intercept.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a single numerical value representing the intercept of the trained regression model. For example, if \texttt{reg.intercept\_} returns \texttt{89.5}, it means that when all independent variables are zero, the predicted value of the dependent variable is 89.5.

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{37}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
np.float64(33.4100009104359)
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Prediction Using Multiple Linear Regression Equation}       
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{38}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} cross checking the predicted value with the value obtaned from the}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} multiple linear regression equation as given below}

\PY{l+m+mi}{160}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.33059217} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mf}{1.61053246} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{o}{*}\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mf}{6.20772074} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+m+mf}{33.410000910435855}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This step cross-checks the predicted value obtained from the Multiple Linear Regression model by manually computing the regression equation. The regression equation uses the coefficients obtained during model training for each feature and adds the intercept term. In this example, the predicted value is calculated as:
\[
\text{Predicted Value} = (160 \times 0.33059217) + (10 \times 1.61053246) + (5 \times -6.20772074) + 33.410000910435855
\]
This helps verify that the model prediction aligns with the mathematical formulation of the regression model.

\subsection*{Output}
The computed output of the equation represents the predicted value of the dependent variable based on the provided feature values. In this case, the calculation yields the numeric value corresponding to the model's prediction for the given input features. Yes it is the same , Congratulations!!!

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsection{converting categorical values to numeric
values}\label{converting-categorical-values-to-numeric-values}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{42}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{sex}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{sex}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{category}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{sex}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{sex}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cat}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{codes}
\PY{n}{df}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In many Machine Learning algorithms, categorical variables need to be converted into numerical values. The code converts the \texttt{sex} column in the dataset into a categorical type and then encodes it as numeric codes. This transformation assigns integer values to each unique category (for example, 0 for 'female' and 1 for 'male'), enabling the regression model to process the feature effectively.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the dataset with the \texttt{sex} column converted to numeric codes. All other columns remain unchanged. The \texttt{sex} column now contains integer values representing the original categories.

\subsection{Encoding Categorical Variables}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{43}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{smoker}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{smoker}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{category}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{smoker}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{smoker}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cat}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{codes}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{region}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{region}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{category}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{region}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{region}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cat}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{codes}
\PY{n}{df}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
Categorical variables in the dataset, such as \texttt{smoker} and \texttt{region}, cannot be directly used in numerical computations for Machine Learning models. To handle this, the variables are first converted to the \texttt{category} data type and then encoded into numerical codes using \texttt{cat.codes}. This process assigns a unique integer to each category, allowing the regression model to process these features effectively.

\subsection*{Output}

\subsection{Checking Missing Values in the Dataset}
 
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{44}{\boxspacing}

\PY{n}{df}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{df.isnull().sum()} function is used to identify missing values in the dataset. It checks each column for null (NaN) entries and returns the total number of missing values per column. This step is crucial in data preprocessing because missing values can negatively affect the performance of machine learning models. After checking for nulls, displaying \texttt{df} shows the entire dataset including all columns and rows.

\subsection*{Output}
The output lists the count of missing values for each column in the dataset. A zero indicates no missing values in that column. Following this, the full dataset is displayed, allowing inspection of data entries for correctness and completeness.

\subsection{Feature Selection using DataFrame Drop}        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{45}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{x} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{charges}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The code \texttt{x = df.drop(columns='charges')} is used to create a new DataFrame \texttt{x} that contains all the features from the original dataset \texttt{df} except the target column \texttt{charges}. This step is performed in preparation for training a regression model, where \texttt{x} represents the independent variables (features) and \texttt{charges} represents the dependent variable (target).

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a DataFrame \texttt{x} containing all columns of the original dataset except \texttt{charges}. This DataFrame is ready to be used as the input for the regression model.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{charges}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}

This variable represents the dependent variable that the model will learn to predict based on the input features.

\subsection*{Output}

\subsection{Train-Test Split}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{train\_test\_split} function from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection} is used to divide the dataset into training and testing subsets. Here, \texttt{x} and \texttt{y} represent the independent and dependent variables, respectively. The parameter \texttt{test\_size = 0.3} specifies that 30\% of the data will be used for testing, while the remaining 70\% is used for training the model. \texttt{random\_state = 0} ensures reproducibility of the split by initializing the random number generator to a fixed state.

\subsection*{Output}
The output includes four arrays:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{xtrain} – Features for training the model.
    \item \texttt{xtest} – Features for testing the model.
    \item \texttt{ytrain} – Target values corresponding to \texttt{xtrain}.
    \item \texttt{ytest} – Target values corresponding to \texttt{xtest}.
\end{itemize}
These subsets are used to train the regression model and evaluate its performance on unseen data.

        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{49}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{xtest}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytest} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{x}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.3}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Simple Linear Regression Model Fitting}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{50}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{linear\PYZus{}model} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{LinearRegression}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{51}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LinearRegression} \PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{52}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{52}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
LinearRegression()
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{53}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{c} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{intercept\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{54}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{c}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The code implements a Simple Linear Regression model using the \texttt{LinearRegression} class from \texttt{sklearn.linear\_model}. The model object \texttt{lr} is created and trained using the \texttt{fit()} method with training features \texttt{xtrain} and target variable \texttt{ytrain}. After training, the model's intercept (the constant term in the regression equation) is stored in variable \texttt{c}. The intercept represents the predicted value of the target when all independent features are zero.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the intercept value of the trained regression model. For example, if \texttt{c = 50000}, it indicates that the base predicted value of the target variable is 50,000 when all independent variables are zero. This intercept is a key component of the regression equation: 
\[
\hat{y} = c + m \cdot x
\]
where \(m\) is the slope and \(x\) is the independent feature.
            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{54}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
np.float64(-11827.733141795718)
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Model Coefficients}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{55}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{m} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{coef\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{56}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{m}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In Linear Regression, the coefficients represent the weight of each independent variable in predicting the dependent variable. The code \texttt{m = lr.coef\_} extracts the coefficients learned by the trained Linear Regression model \texttt{lr}. Each value in \texttt{m} corresponds to the change in the target variable for a one-unit change in the respective feature, assuming all other features remain constant.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is an array of numerical values representing the slope(s) of the regression line(s) for the feature(s) used in the model. For example, in Simple Linear Regression, this will be a single value, while in Multiple Linear Regression, there will be one value for each independent variable.

\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{56}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
array([  256.5772619 ,   -49.39232379,   329.02381564,   479.08499828,
       23400.28378787,  -276.31576201])
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
 
 
 
 \subsection{Prediction using Trained Linear Regression Model}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{57}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Explanation}
After training the Linear Regression model, predictions are generated for the training dataset using the \texttt{predict()} method. The variable \texttt{y\_pred\_train} stores the predicted values of the dependent variable corresponding to the input features in \texttt{xtrain}. This step allows us to compare the predicted values with the actual target values to evaluate the model's performance on the training data.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays an array of predicted values for each record in the training dataset. These values represent the model's estimation of the target variable based on the learned relationship from the training data. For example:

\begin{verbatim}
[ 45000.12, 56000.34, 62000.45, 48000.00, ... ]
\end{verbatim}

 \subsection{Scatter Plot of Actual vs Predicted Values}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{59}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{plt}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{scatter}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}plt.xlabel(\PYZsq{}Actual Charges\PYZsq{})}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}plt.ylabel(\PYZsq{}Predicted Charges\PYZsq{})}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Actual Charges}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{color}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{red}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Predicted Charges}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{color}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{black}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code visualizes the relationship between actual and predicted values of the target variable using a scatter plot. The independent variable on the x-axis represents the actual values (\texttt{y\_train}), while the dependent variable on the y-axis represents the predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred\_train}) from the regression model. Color-coded axis labels are used to enhance readability. Such a plot helps in evaluating the model's performance: points closer to the diagonal line indicate better predictions.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a scatter plot where each point represents a data instance. The x-axis shows the actual target values, and the y-axis shows the predicted values. Ideally, points lying close to the line \texttt{y = x} indicate accurate predictions. The plot provides a visual assessment of the regression model's accuracy.

    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{Lab-02/output_64_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Predicted Charges}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\subsection{R-squared Score Evaluation}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{61}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{r2\PYZus{}score}
\PY{n}{r2\PYZus{}score} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The R-squared score is a statistical metric used to evaluate the performance of a regression model. It indicates the proportion of variance in the dependent variable that is predictable from the independent variable(s). The function \texttt{r2\_score} from \texttt{sklearn.metrics} compares the actual values (\texttt{ytrain}) with the predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred\_train}) to calculate this score. An R-squared value closer to 1 indicates a better fit of the model to the data.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a single numerical value representing the R-squared score for the training dataset. For example, if the output is \texttt{0.85}, it means that 85\% of the variance in the dependent variable is explained by the model.

\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{61}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
0.7306840408360217
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Prediction using Trained Linear Regression Model}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{62}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{lr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{xtest}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The code \texttt{y\_pred\_test = lr.predict(xtest)} uses the trained Linear Regression model \texttt{lr} to predict the values of the dependent variable for the test dataset \texttt{xtest}. The \texttt{predict()} method applies the learned coefficients and intercept from the training phase to the new input data, generating predicted outputs. This step is essential for evaluating the model's performance on unseen data.

\subsection{R-squared Score Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{r2\_score} function from \texttt{sklearn.metrics} is used to evaluate the performance of a regression model. It measures how well the predicted values match the actual values. The R-squared value ranges from 0 to 1, where 1 indicates perfect prediction and 0 indicates that the model does not explain any variability in the target variable. In this code, \texttt{r2\_score(y\_test, y\_pred\_test)} calculates the R-squared score for the test dataset predictions, providing a quantitative assessment of the model's accuracy.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a single numerical value between 0 and 1 representing the coefficient of determination (R-squared) for the regression model. Higher values indicate better model performance.

\chapter{K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN)}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-03: K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN)}

\section*{Objective}

The objective of this Machine Learning lab is to study and implement the K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) algorithm for both classification and regression tasks using real-world datasets. This lab aims to understand the working principle of KNN based on distance measurement and neighborhood similarity. The experiment involves applying KNN as a classifier to categorize data into distinct classes and as a regressor to predict continuous values. Additionally, the lab focuses on evaluating model performance using appropriate metrics and analyzing how the choice of neighbors influences prediction accuracy.


\section*{Dataset Description}

Three different datasets are used in this lab to demonstrate the application of regression algorithms.

\subsection*{Irish Dataset}
The \texttt{IRIS.csv} dataset is a well-known classification dataset containing measurements of iris flowers. The dataset includes multiple numerical attributes representing flower characteristics, and a categorical class label indicating the species of the iris plant. This dataset is suitable for demonstrating the working principle of the KNN classification algorithm.v

\subsection*{Car Prices Dataset}
The \texttt{carprices.csv} dataset contains automobile-related attributes used to predict car prices. The dataset includes multiple numerical features representing car characteristics and a continuous target variable representing the price. This dataset is used to demonstrate the effectiveness of KNN in regression problems.
   

\section{KNN as Classifier}\label{knn-as-classifier-as-regressor-in-another-program}

\subsection{Assigning Weather Feature Values for KNN Classifier}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, a feature variable named \texttt{weather} is created and assigned a list of categorical values representing different weather conditions such as \texttt{Sunny}, \texttt{Overcast}, and \texttt{Rainy}. Each value in the list corresponds to an individual data instance in the dataset. This feature serves as an input attribute for Machine Learning algorithms and is typically used in classification problems to analyze the impact of weather conditions on the target variable.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Assigning Feature and local variables}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}First Feature}
\PY{n}{weather} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sunny}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sunny}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Overcast}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rainy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rainy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rainy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Overcast}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sunny}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sunny}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rainy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sunny}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Overcast}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Overcast}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rainy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Second Feature}
\PY{n}{temp}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Hot}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Hot}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Hot}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Cool}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Cool}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Cool}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Cool}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Hot}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Yes}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{No}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{p}{]}

\subsection{Label Encoding of Categorical Data}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, categorical string data is converted into numerical form using the \texttt{LabelEncoder} class from the \texttt{sklearn.preprocessing} module. A Label Encoder object is created and applied to the \texttt{weather} feature using the \texttt{fit\_transform()} method. This method assigns a unique numerical value to each distinct categorical label, enabling Machine Learning algorithms to process non-numeric data effectively.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{preprocessing}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a numerical array where each categorical value in the \texttt{weather} feature is replaced by a corresponding integer label. These encoded values represent the original categories in numeric form.

\subsection{Encoding Categorical String Labels}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}col} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{le}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{play}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{temp\PYZus{}encoded}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, categorical string labels are converted into numerical form using label encoding. The \texttt{fit\_transform()} method of the label encoder is applied to the \texttt{temp} feature and the target variable \texttt{play}. This process assigns a unique numerical value to each distinct categorical label. Converting string labels to numbers is necessary because most Machine Learning algorithms require numerical input for training and prediction.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the encoded numerical values of the \texttt{temp} feature stored in \texttt{temp\_encoded}. Each unique string category is represented by a corresponding integer value.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{features}

The \texttt{zip()} function is used to pair corresponding elements from both lists, and the resulting pairs are converted into a list of tuples. Each tuple represents a single data point containing multiple features, which is required as input for Machine Learning algorithms such as KNN.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a list of tuples where each tuple contains the encoded weather value and the encoded temperature value for a particular data instance. This combined feature list is used as the input feature matrix for model training.

\subsection{Training K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Classifier}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{10}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{neighbors} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier}
\PY{n}{model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{features}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}col}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code initializes and trains a K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Classifier using the \texttt{KNeighborsClassifier} class from the \texttt{sklearn.neighbors} module. The parameter \texttt{n\_neighbors=3} specifies that the model will consider the three nearest data points to determine the class of a new sample. The \texttt{fit()} method is used to train the classifier using the feature set (\texttt{features}) and the corresponding class labels (\texttt{label\_col}). KNN is a distance-based algorithm that stores the training data and makes predictions based on similarity.

\subsection*{Output}
The output confirms that the KNN Classifier has been successfully created with three neighbors. The trained model object \texttt{KNeighborsClassifier(n\_neighbors=3)} is displayed, indicating that the model is ready for making predictions.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\PY{n}{predicted}

The \texttt{predict()} function takes the input features in the form of a two-dimensional array and returns the predicted output based on the learned patterns from the training data. In this case, the input \texttt{[1, 0]} represents a single data instance with two feature values.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the predicted value or class label generated by the model for the given input data. The result depends on the model type and the patterns learned during training.

\section{KNN Classifier in Iris Dataset}
\subsection{Importing Required Libraries}


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{numpy} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{np}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{pandas} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{plt}

\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{datasets} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{load\PYZus{}iris}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{preprocessing} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{StandardScaler}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{neighbors} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{,} 
\PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code imports all the necessary Python libraries required to implement the K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Classifier using the Iris dataset. NumPy and Pandas are used for numerical computation and data handling. Matplotlib is used for data visualization. The Iris dataset is loaded using \texttt{load\_iris()} from \texttt{sklearn.datasets}. The dataset is split into training and testing sets using \texttt{train\_test\_split()}. Feature scaling is performed using \texttt{StandardScaler} to normalize the data. The \texttt{KNeighborsClassifier} is used to build the classification model, and evaluation metrics such as accuracy score, confusion matrix, and classification report are imported to assess model performance.

\subsection{Loading and Preparing the Iris Dataset}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{iris} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{load\PYZus{}iris}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{iris}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{data}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{iris}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{target}

\PY{n}{df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{iris}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{feature\PYZus{}names}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{target}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{y}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the Iris dataset is loaded using the \texttt{load\_iris()} function from the \texttt{sklearn.datasets} module. The feature matrix \texttt{X} contains the numerical measurements of iris flowers, while the target vector \texttt{y} contains the corresponding class labels. A Pandas DataFrame is then created using the feature data with appropriate column names. The target labels are added as a new column named \texttt{target}. This structured DataFrame format facilitates data inspection and further preprocessing required for the KNN classification algorithm.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the DataFrame, showing the feature values along with the corresponding target class labels for the Iris dataset.

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
