\subsection{Train-Test Split in KNN Classifier}


        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(} \PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42} \PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{scaler} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{StandardScaler}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{knn} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{knn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
KNeighborsClassifier()
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this code, the dataset is split into training and testing sets using \texttt{train\_test\_split}, with 80\% for training and 20\% for testing. The features are standardized using \texttt{StandardScaler} to normalize the data, which improves KNN performance by ensuring all features contribute equally. A KNN classifier is created with 5 neighbors (\texttt{n\_neighbors=5}) and is trained on the scaled training set using the \texttt{fit} method. Finally, the trained model predicts the labels for the test set using \texttt{predict}, producing the array of predicted class labels.

\subsection*{Output}
The output of the code is the predicted class labels (\texttt{y\_pred}) for the test dataset. It will be an array of species names (or class labels) corresponding to each sample in the test set. For example:
\begin{verbatim}
array([1, 0, 2, 1, ...])
\end{verbatim}
This output can be used to evaluate the classifier's accuracy against the actual test labels (\texttt{y\_test}).

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
array([1, 0, 2, 1, 1, 0, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 0, 2,
       0, 2, 2, 2, 2, 2, 0, 0])
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection{KNN Classifier Evaluation Metrics}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{accuracy} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Accuracy:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{accuracy}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Confusion Matrix:}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Classification Report:}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
After training the K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Classifier, the model's performance is evaluated using three metrics:

1. \textbf{Accuracy:} Measures the proportion of correctly classified instances in the test dataset. It provides an overall assessment of the classifier's performance.

2. \textbf{Confusion Matrix:} A table that summarizes the counts of true positives, true negatives, false positives, and false negatives for each class. It helps to identify which classes are being misclassified.

3. \textbf{Classification Report:} Provides detailed metrics for each class, including precision, recall, and F1-score. Precision indicates how many predicted positives are actually correct, recall indicates how many actual positives are correctly predicted, and F1-score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall.

The code computes these metrics for the test dataset predictions (\texttt{y\_pred}) and compares them with the true labels (\texttt{y\_test}).

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays:

\begin{itemize}
  \item The \textbf{Accuracy} of the KNN Classifier on the test data.
  \item The \textbf{Confusion Matrix}, showing the count of correct and incorrect classifications for each class.
  \item The \textbf{Classification Report}, listing precision, recall, and F1-score for all classes.
\end{itemize}
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Accuracy: 1.0

Confusion Matrix:
 [[10  0  0]
 [ 0  9  0]
 [ 0  0 11]]

Classification Report:
               precision    recall  f1-score   support

           0       1.00      1.00      1.00        10
           1       1.00      1.00      1.00         9
           2       1.00      1.00      1.00        11

    accuracy                           1.00        30
   macro avg       1.00      1.00      1.00        30
weighted avg       1.00      1.00      1.00        30

    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{KNN Error Rate Analysis}


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{error\PYZus{}rate} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{k} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{21}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{knn} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KNeighborsClassifier}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{k}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{knn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{pred\PYZus{}k} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{error\PYZus{}rate}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{pred\PYZus{}k} \PY{o}{!=} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{plot}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+m+mi}{21}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{error\PYZus{}rate}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{marker}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{o}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{K Value}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Error Rate}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{K Value vs Error Rate}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code calculates the error rate of a K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) classifier for different values of $k$ (number of neighbors). The process involves the following steps:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item An empty list \texttt{error\_rate} is initialized to store the mean error for each $k$.
    \item A loop runs for $k$ values from 1 to 20.
    \item For each $k$, a \texttt{KNeighborsClassifier} is created with the current number of neighbors.
    \item The classifier is trained using the training data (\texttt{X\_train, y\_train}).
    \item Predictions are made on the test set (\texttt{X\_test}), and the mean error rate (fraction of incorrect predictions) is computed and appended to the \texttt{error\_rate} list.
    \item After the loop, a line plot is created showing the relationship between $k$ and the error rate.
    \item The x-axis represents the $k$ value, the y-axis represents the error rate, and the title summarizes the plot purpose.
\end{enumerate}

This analysis helps in selecting the optimal $k$ value that minimizes classification error, which is crucial for improving the performance of the KNN classifier.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a line plot showing the error rate corresponding to each $k$ value from 1 to 20. Typically, the plot allows the identification of the $k$ value with the lowest error rate. Example:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $k$ = 1: high variance, potential overfitting
    \item $k$ around 5-7: lowest error rate, optimal choice
    \item $k$ > 10: increased bias, error may rise
\end{itemize}

The plot visually guides the selection of an appropriate $k$ for the KNN classifier to achieve better generalization on the test data.

    \end{center}

\section{KNN AS REGRESSOR}\label{knn-as-regressor}
\subsection{Importing Libraries}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 1: Import Libraries}
\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n+nn}{pandas} \PY{k}{as} \PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{neighbors} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{KNeighborsRegressor}
\PY{k+kn}{from} \PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics} \PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{mean\PYZus{}squared\PYZus{}error}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, we import the necessary Python libraries required for implementing the K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) regression model:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{pandas} is used for data manipulation and analysis.
    \item \texttt{train\_test\_split} from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection} is used to split the dataset into training and testing sets.
    \item \texttt{KNeighborsRegressor} from \texttt{sklearn.neighbors} is used to create the KNN regression model.
    \item \texttt{mean\_squared\_error} from \texttt{sklearn.metrics} is used to evaluate the model performance by calculating the mean squared error between predicted and actual values.
\end{itemize}
This step ensures that all the tools required for building, training, and evaluating the KNN regression model are available.
\subsection{Creating DataFrame}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 2: Create the DataFrame}
\PY{n}{data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{id}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{7}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{9}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{25}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{30}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{35}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{40}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{45}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{50}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{55}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{60}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{65}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{70}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{height}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{170}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{160}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{180}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{165}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{175}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{160}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{170}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{180}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{165}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{175}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{weight}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{65}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{60}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{70}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{75}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{55}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{70}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{85}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{60}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{75}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
\PY{n}{df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, a pandas DataFrame is created using a dictionary of lists. The dictionary contains sample data for 10 individuals with attributes \texttt{id}, \texttt{age}, \texttt{height}, and \texttt{weight}. The pandas \texttt{DataFrame} constructor is used to organize this structured data into a tabular format. This dataset will later be used as input for the KNN regression model to predict numerical target values based on similarity between features.
\newpage
\subsection*{Output}
\begin{verbatim}
   id  age  height  weight
0   1   25     170      65
1   2   30     160      60
2   3   35     180      80
3   4   40     165      70
4   5   45     175      75
5   6   50     160      55
6   7   55     170      70
7   8   60     180      85
8   9   65     165      60
9  10   70     175      75
\end{verbatim}


\subsection{Preparing the Data for Regression}
\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 3: Prepare the Data}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Features (independent variables)}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{height}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Target (dependent variable)}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{weight}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the dataset is prepared for the regression model. The independent variables (features) are selected from the dataframe \texttt{df} and stored in variable \texttt{X}. In this example, the features are \texttt{age} and \texttt{height}. The dependent variable (target) \texttt{y} is the column \texttt{weight}, which we aim to predict using the regression model. This separation of features and target is essential for supervised learning.

\subsection{Training and Prediction}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 4: Split the Data}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 5: Train the KNN Regressor}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Initialize the KNN Regressor}
\PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KNeighborsRegressor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train the model}
\PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{KNeighborsRegressor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the dataset is split into training and testing sets using the \texttt{train\_test\_split} function from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection}. 80\% of the data is used for training and 20\% for testing. 

The \texttt{KNeighborsRegressor} model is then initialized with \texttt{n\_neighbors=3}, meaning the prediction for a data point will be based on the average of the three nearest neighbors in the feature space. The model is trained on the training data (\texttt{X\_train} and \texttt{y\_train}) using the \texttt{fit} method. 

This step prepares the model to predict continuous target values based on the learned patterns from the training set.

\subsection*{Output}
\begin{verbatim}
KNeighborsRegressor(n_neighbors=3)
\end{verbatim}
The output indicates that a KNN Regressor has been successfully created and trained with 3 neighbors.
\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
KNeighborsRegressor(n\_neighbors=3)
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
    
\subsection{KNN Regressor Predictions}

    
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 6: Make Predictions}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict weights for the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Display predictions}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted Weights:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the trained K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Regressor model is used to predict the target values for the test dataset. The \texttt{predict()} method computes the predicted values based on the average of the target values of the K nearest neighbors for each test instance. This allows us to estimate continuous outcomes, such as car prices or weights, using the features from the test set.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the predicted values for the test dataset. 
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Predicted Weights: [76.66666667 63.33333333]
    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{Model Evaluation using Mean Squared Error (MSE)}


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 7: Evaluate the Model}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Calculate Mean Squared Error}
\PY{n}{mse} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{mean\PYZus{}squared\PYZus{}error}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mean Squared Error:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{mse}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the performance of the regression model is evaluated using the Mean Squared Error (MSE). MSE measures the average of the squares of the differences between the actual target values (\texttt{y\_test}) and the predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred}). A lower MSE indicates that the model's predictions are closer to the actual values, reflecting better performance.

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a single numeric value representing the Mean Squared Error.
\begin{verbatim}
Mean Squared Error: 144.44444444444454
\end{verbatim}


\subsection{Predicting Weight for New Data using KNN Regressor}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 8: Predict Weight for New Data}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} New input data}
\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{32}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{172}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict weight}
\PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}weight} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted Weight for Age=32, Height=172:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}weight}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, we use the trained KNN regressor model to predict the weight of a person based on new input data. The input features are age and height, provided as a two-dimensional array \texttt{new\_data = [[32, 172]]}. The \texttt{predict()} method of the KNN regressor is then called with this input to estimate the corresponding weight. This demonstrates the application of the KNN model for regression tasks on unseen data.

\subsection*{Output}
The predicted weight value is printed to the console. For example:

\begin{verbatim}
Predicted Weight for Age=32, Height=172: 71.66666666666667
\end{verbatim}

Here, \texttt{70.45} is the predicted weight for a person of age 32 and height 172 cm according to the trained KNN regression model.

 \subsection{Preparing Data for KNN Regression}



      
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 3: Prepare the Data}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Features (independent variables)}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Mileage}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Target (dependent variable)}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sell Price}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the dataset is prepared for KNN regression. The independent variables (features) selected are \texttt{Mileage} and \texttt{Age} of the cars, which are stored in the variable \texttt{X}. The dependent variable (target) is the \texttt{Sell Price} of the cars, which is stored in the variable \texttt{y}. This separation of features and target is essential for training a supervised regression model.


\subsection{KNN Regressor Training}


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 4: Split the Data}

\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 5: Train the KNN Regressor}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Initialize the KNN Regressor}
\PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KNeighborsRegressor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train the model}
\PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{KNeighborsRegressor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}neighbors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the dataset is split into training and testing sets using the \texttt{train\_test\_split} function, with 80\% of the data used for training and 20\% for testing. The \texttt{KNeighborsRegressor} is then initialized with 3 neighbors, and the model is trained using the training data (\texttt{X\_train} and \texttt{y\_train}). The \texttt{fit} function allows the KNN regressor to memorize the training data for future predictions based on the nearest neighbors principle.

\subsection*{Output}
The trained KNN regressor object is created:
\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
KNeighborsRegressor(n\_neighbors=3)
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

This object can now be used to make predictions on the test dataset (\texttt{X\_test}) to evaluate the model's performance.  
\newpage
      
\subsection{Making Predictions}

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 6: Make Predictions}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict weights for the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Display predictions}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted Sell Price:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the trained K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) Regressor is used to predict the target values for the test dataset. The \texttt{predict()} method of the KNN Regressor takes the test features (\texttt{X\_test}) and computes predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred}) based on the average of the target values of the K nearest neighbors from the training set. This allows the model to estimate continuous values such as car prices based on similarity to known data points.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the predicted values for the test set.
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Predicted Sell Price: [20766.66666667 42166.66666667 30366.66666667
24766.66666667]
    \end{Verbatim}
These values represent the estimated car prices for the corresponding test inputs.

\subsection{Model Evaluation using Mean Squared Error (MSE)}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 7: Evaluate the Model}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Calculate Mean Squared Error}
\PY{n}{mse} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{mean\PYZus{}squared\PYZus{}error}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mean Squared Error:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{mse}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
Mean Squared Error (MSE) is a common metric used to evaluate the performance of regression models. It measures the average of the squares of the differences between the actual target values (\texttt{y\_test}) and the predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred}) generated by the model. Lower MSE values indicate better predictive performance, as the predicted values are closer to the actual values. In this step, the MSE is calculated for the test dataset to assess how well the regression model generalizes to unseen data.

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the numerical value of the Mean Squared Error for the test dataset.
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Mean Squared Error: 31901111.111111097
    \end{Verbatim}
This value quantifies the average squared deviation of predictions from the actual target values.
\newpage

\subsection{Predicting Sell Price using KNN Regressor}
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{9}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Step 8: Predict Sell Price for New Data}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} New input data}
\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{69000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict weight}
\PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}price} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{knn\PYZus{}regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted Sell Price for Using Age=6, Mileage=69000, Sell\PYZus{}Price=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}price}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, we predict the selling price of a car using the trained K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) regressor. The model takes a new input sample containing the car's mileage and age. The \texttt{predict()} method computes the average of the target values (sell prices) of the k-nearest neighbors in the training dataset for this new input. This allows us to estimate the car's selling price based on similarity to previously seen data points.

\chapter{Label and One-Hot Encoding}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-04: Label and One-Hot Encoding}

\section*{Objective}
The objective of this experiment is to understand and implement data preprocessing and unsupervised learning techniques in Machine Learning. Specifically, this lab focuses on applying Label Encoding and One-Hot Encoding to transform categorical data into numerical format suitable for machine learning models. Additionally, the experiment aims to apply the K-Means Clustering algorithm to group similar data points based on feature similarity.

The goals of this experiment are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item To learn how to convert categorical variables into numerical representations using Label Encoding and One-Hot Encoding.
    \item To analyze the impact of encoding techniques on dataset structure.
    \item To implement the K-Means clustering algorithm for unsupervised learning.
    \item To identify natural groupings (clusters) in customer data based on features such as income and spending behavior.
    \item To visualize clusters and interpret patterns in the dataset.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Dataset Description}

\subsection*{Encoding Dataset (encoding.csv)}
The dataset \texttt{encoding.csv} is used to demonstrate categorical data preprocessing techniques. It contains one or more categorical features such as gender, country, or other non-numeric attributes.

Key characteristics:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Contains categorical variables that cannot be directly used in machine learning models.
    \item Used to apply Label Encoding, where each category is assigned a unique integer.
    \item Used to apply One-Hot Encoding, where categorical values are converted into binary columns.
    \item Helps in understanding how different encoding techniques affect data representation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Mall Customer Dataset (Mall\_Customers.csv)}
The dataset \texttt{Mall\_Customers.csv} is used for K-Means clustering. It contains customer information collected from a shopping mall. Typical attributes include:
CustomerID, Gender, Age, Annual Income (k\$), Spending Score (1-100)

%\newpage

\section{Label and Onehot Encoding}

\subsection{Label Encoding on Categorical Data}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the dataset \texttt{encoding.csv} is loaded using the \texttt{pandas} library. The dataset contains categorical features such as \texttt{Color} and \texttt{Size}, which cannot be directly used in machine learning models.

To convert these categorical values into numerical form, \texttt{LabelEncoder} from \texttt{sklearn.preprocessing} is used. Label Encoding assigns a unique integer value to each category in a column.

For example:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{Color}: Red, Green, Blue $\rightarrow$ 2, 1, 0 (mapping may vary)
    \item \texttt{Size}: Small, Medium, Large $\rightarrow$ 2, 1, 0 (mapping may vary)
\end{itemize}

Two new columns are created:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{Color\_LabelEncoded}
    \item \texttt{Size\_LabelEncoded}
\end{itemize}

These columns contain the numeric representation of the original categorical values.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pandas}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{preprocessing}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{OneHotEncoder}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load the dataset}
\PY{n}{df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{read\PYZus{}csv}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{encoding.csv}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
After applying Label Encoding, the dataset will include additional columns with encoded values. A output may look like:



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Apply Label Encoding (Converting categorical columns into numeric labels)}
\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}enc} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Color\PYZus{}LabelEncoded}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}enc}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Color}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Size\PYZus{}LabelEncoded}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}enc}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Size}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{n}\\{df}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
   ID  Color    Size  Price  Color\_LabelEncoded  Size\_LabelEncoded
0   1    Red   Small   10.5                   2                  2
1   2   Blue  Medium   15.0                   0                  1
2   3  Green   Large   12.3                   1                  0
3   4   Blue   Small   14.2                   0                  2
4   5    Red   Large   13.1                   2                  0
5   6  Green  Medium   16.8                   1                  1
6   7    Red   Small   11.5                   2                  2
7   8   Blue   Large   17.3                   0                  0
8   9  Green  Medium   14.9                   1                  1
9  10    Red   Large   15.7                   2                  0
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
        
\subsection{One-Hot Encoding of Categorical Features}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, One-Hot Encoding is applied to the categorical columns \texttt{Color} and \texttt{Size} using the \texttt{pd.get\_dummies()} function from the pandas library. One-Hot Encoding converts each unique category value into a separate binary column. Instead of assigning a single numeric label (as in Label Encoding), it creates multiple columns, where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Each column represents one category
    \item Values are either 0 or 1
    \item 1 indicates the presence of that category, and 0 indicates absence
\end{itemize}

The parameter \texttt{columns=['Color','Size']} specifies which columns to encode, and \texttt{prefix} is used to name the new columns clearly.

As a result, the original \texttt{Color} and \texttt{Size} columns are removed and replaced with multiple new binary columns such as:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{Color\_Red, Color\_Green, Color\_Blue}
    \item \texttt{Size\_Small, Size\_Medium, Size\_Large}
\end{itemize} 

      
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Apply One\PYZhy{}Hot Encoding (Creating binary columns)}
\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}onehot} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get\PYZus{}dummies}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Color}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Size}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{prefix}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Color}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Size}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}onehot}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
After applying One-Hot Encoding, the dataset will look like this:
Each row now represents categorical information using binary (0/1) values, making the dataset suitable for machine learning models.

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
   ID  Price  Color\_LabelEncoded  Size\_LabelEncoded  Color\_Blue  Color\_Green  \textbackslash{}

1   2   15.0                   0                  1        True        False
2   3   12.3                   1                  0       False         True
3   4   14.2                   0                  2        True        False
4   5   13.1                   2                  0       False        False
5   6   16.8                   1                  1       False         True
6   7   11.5                   2                  2       False        False
7   8   17.3                   0                  0        True        False
8   9   14.9                   1                  1       False         True
9  10   15.7                   2                  0       False        False

   Color\_Red  Size\_Large  Size\_Medium  Size\_Small
0       True       False        False        True
1      False       False         True       False
2      False        True        False       False

5      False       False         True       False
6       True       False        False        True
7      False        True        False       False
8      False       False         True       False
9       True        True        False       False
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
        
\subsection{Convert One-Hot Encoded Boolean Values to Integer}

\subsection*{Explanation}
After applying One-Hot Encoding, the dataset \texttt{df\_onehot} may contain boolean values (\texttt{True}/\texttt{False}). To make the dataset more suitable for machine learning models, these boolean values are converted into integer format using \texttt{astype(int)}.

This converts:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{True} $\rightarrow$ 1
    \item \texttt{False} $\rightarrow$ 0
\end{itemize}

As a result, all columns in \texttt{df\_onehot} become numeric.        

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert boolean values to integers}
\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}onehot} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}onehot}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}\\\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}onehot}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
After conversion, the dataset will contain only integer values (0 and 1).

\section{K-means-clustering}\label{k-means-clustering}
\lhead{Lab-04: K-means Clustering}
\subsection{Dataset Loading}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the required libraries such as \texttt{pandas}, \texttt{numpy}, \texttt{seaborn}, and \texttt{matplotlib} are imported for data handling and visualization. The dataset \texttt{Mall\_Customers.csv} is then loaded into a DataFrame using \texttt{pd.read\_csv()}.

This dataset typically contains customer information such as CustomerID, Gender, Age, Annual Income, and Spending Score. Loading the dataset is the initial step before applying K-Means clustering to group customers based on similar characteristics.

The parameter \texttt{inplace=True} ensures that the changes are applied directly to the original DataFrame.

\subsection{Pairplot Visualization using Seaborn}

\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{seaborn.pairplot()} function is used to visualize pairwise relationships between multiple numerical variables in a dataset. In this case, the selected features are \texttt{age}, \texttt{income}, and \texttt{score}.

This function automatically generates:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Scatter plots for each pair of variables.
    \item Histograms (or KDE plots) along the diagonal to show distribution of each feature.
\end{itemize}

It helps in understanding correlations, patterns, and distributions among features, which is useful for exploratory data analysis (EDA).

        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{9}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a grid of plots where, Each off-diagonal plot shows the relationship between two variables (e.g., age vs income, income vs score), Diagonal plots show the distribution of each individual feature. The final output is a matrix-style scatter plot visualization (pairplot) displaying relationships between all selected features.

\subsection{K-Means Clustering Implementation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the K-Means clustering algorithm is applied using the \texttt{sklearn.cluster} library. The dataset is grouped based on two features: \texttt{score} and \texttt{income}. The number of clusters is set to 5, meaning the algorithm will divide the data into five distinct groups.

The K-Means algorithm works by:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Randomly initializing 5 cluster centroids.
    \item Assigning each data point to the nearest centroid.
    \item Updating centroids based on the mean of assigned points.
    \item Repeating the process until convergence.
\end{itemize}

After fitting the model, the cluster centroids are extracted using \texttt{cluster\_centers\_}, which represent the central point of each cluster.
   
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{10}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{cluster}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{cluster}
\PY{n}{kmeans} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cluster}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{KMeans}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}clusters} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{11}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{kmeans} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{12}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}Finding out the centroids}
\PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cluster\PYZus{}centers\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The output of this step is the coordinates of the 5 cluster centroids in the feature space \texttt{(score, income)}. Each row represents the center of a cluster, showing the average \texttt{score} and \texttt{income} values for that group.
            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{12}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
array([[82.12820513, 86.53846154],
       [49.51851852, 55.2962963 ],
       [79.36363636, 25.72727273],
       [17.11428571, 88.2       ],
       [20.91304348, 26.30434783]])
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
 
\subsection{K-Means Model Training and Cluster Centers Extraction}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the K-Means clustering algorithm is applied to the dataset using two selected features: \texttt{income} and \texttt{score}. The model is trained using the \texttt{fit()} function, which groups the data into clusters based on similarity between data points.

After training the model, the \texttt{cluster\_centers\_} attribute is used to retrieve the centroid (center point) of each cluster. These centroids represent the average position of all data points within each cluster and help in understanding the grouping pattern of the dataset.

       
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{13}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{kmeans} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{14}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cluster\PYZus{}centers\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The output of \texttt{kmeans.cluster\_centers\_} is a set of numerical values representing the center of each cluster in the feature space:

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{14}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
array([[26.30434783, 20.91304348],
       [86.53846154, 82.12820513],
       [88.2       , 17.11428571],
       [25.72727273, 79.36363636],
       [55.2962963 , 49.51851852]])
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        

\subsection{Assigning K-Means Cluster Labels to Dataset}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the cluster labels generated by the K-Means algorithm are assigned to the original dataset. The attribute \texttt{kmeans.labels\_} contains the cluster index for each data point after training the model.

These labels represent the cluster group to which each record belongs. By storing them in a new column named \texttt{income\_clusters}, we can easily analyze and visualize the segmented groups within the dataset.

This step is essential for interpreting the clustering results in a structured tabular format.

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{15}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income\PYZus{}clusters}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{labels\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{16}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
After executing the code, a new column \texttt{income\_clusters} is added to the dataset. Each row now includes a cluster label (e.g., 0, 1, 2, etc.), indicating the assigned cluster group.

The updated dataset will look like:

198         199    Male   32     137     18                2

[200 rows x 6 columns]

\subsection{Cluster Value Count using value\_counts()}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This step is used after applying clustering (e.g., K-Means) on the dataset. The column \texttt{income\_clusters} contains the cluster labels assigned to each data point.

The function \texttt{value\_counts()} is used to count how many data points belong to each cluster. This helps in understanding the distribution of customers across different clusters and checking whether the clustering is balanced or not.

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{17}{\boxspacing}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{K-Means Cluster Visualization using Scatter Plot}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code uses Seaborn's \texttt{scatterplot} function to visualize the results of K-Means clustering. The dataset (\texttt{df}) contains customer information where \texttt{score} and \texttt{income} are used as feature variables.

The parameter \texttt{hue='income\_clusters'} is used to color the data points based on the cluster labels generated by the K-Means algorithm. This helps in visually distinguishing different customer groups based on their income and spending behavior.


\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{18}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{seaborn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{scatterplot}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{x}\PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{hue}\PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income\PYZus{}clusters}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The output is a scatter plot where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item X-axis represents \texttt{score} (customer spending score)
    \item Y-axis represents \texttt{income} (customer income)
    \item Different colors represent different clusters formed by K-Means
\end{itemize}

The plot visually shows how customers are grouped into distinct clusters. Customers with similar income and spending patterns appear close together in the same colored group.

\subsection{Scatter Plot for Cluster Visualization}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code uses the Seaborn library to visualize clustering results using a scatter plot. The plot displays the relationship between \texttt{income} and \texttt{score} from the dataset, while also differentiating data points based on their assigned cluster labels stored in the column \texttt{income\_clusters}.

Each cluster is represented by a different color, allowing clear visualization of how customers are grouped based on similarity in income and spending behavior. This helps in understanding the distribution and separation of clusters generated by the K-Means algorithm.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{K-Means Clustering with 2 Clusters}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the K-Means clustering algorithm is applied to the dataset using two features: \texttt{age} and \texttt{score}. The number of clusters is set to 2, meaning the algorithm will divide the data into two distinct groups based on similarity.

The model is first initialized with \texttt{n\_clusters = 2} and then trained using the \texttt{fit()} method on the selected columns. After training, the cluster centers are extracted using \texttt{cluster\_centers\_}, which represent the average position (centroid) of each cluster in the feature space.

    
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{20}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}trying with 2 clusters}
\PY{n}{kmeans} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cluster}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{KMeans}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}clusters} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{21}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{kmeans} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{22}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{kmeans}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cluster\PYZus{}centers\PYZus{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The output of this code is the coordinates of the two cluster centers:

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]

These cluster centers are used to understand how the dataset is grouped into two segments based on similarity in age and score.


\subsection{Assigning Cluster Labels to Dataset}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the output labels generated by the K-Means clustering algorithm are assigned to the original dataset. The attribute \texttt{kmeans.labels\_} contains the cluster ID for each data point after model fitting.

These cluster labels represent which group each record belongs to based on similarity in feature space. The labels are stored in a new column called \texttt{age\_clusters}, allowing easy interpretation and analysis of clustered groups within the dataset.

\subsection{K-Means Clustering (Elbow Method - WCSS Calculation)}

\subsection*{Explanation}
In this step, the K-Means clustering algorithm is applied to determine the optimal number of clusters for the dataset. The model uses two features: \texttt{income} and \texttt{score}.

The process calculates the Within-Cluster Sum of Squares (WCSS) for different values of \(k\) (number of clusters). WCSS measures how compact the clusters are; lower values indicate better clustering.

A loop is used to test cluster values from 1 to 11. For each value of \(k\), the KMeans algorithm is trained, and the inertia (WCSS value) is stored in a list.

This helps in identifying the "elbow point", where increasing clusters no longer significantly reduces WCSS.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{28}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}from sklearn.cluster import kMeans}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{cluster}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{KMeans}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{29}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} to calculate 12 clusters}
\PY{n}{k\PYZus{}range} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+m+mi}{12}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{wcss} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{30}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} implementing for loop for 12 clusters to find out wcss value for each cluster}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} adding wcss values of clusters in wcss array}

\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{k} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{k\PYZus{}range}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{km}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{KMeans}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}clusters} \PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{k}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{km}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{income}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{wcss}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{km}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inertia\PYZus{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output of this step is a list of WCSS values corresponding to different cluster numbers:

\begin{verbatim}
k = 1  -> WCSS = high value
k = 2  -> WCSS decreases
k = 3  -> WCSS decreases further
...
k = 11 -> WCSS becomes relatively stable
\end{verbatim}

These values are later plotted in an Elbow Curve graph to determine the optimal number of clusters. The point where the curve bends (elbow point) indicates the best value of \(k\).


\subsection{Showing the values of WCSS (Within-Cluster Sum of Squares)}
\subsection*{Explanation}
WCSS (Within-Cluster Sum of Squares) is a metric used in K-Means clustering to measure the compactness of clusters. It calculates the sum of squared distances between each data point and the centroid of its assigned cluster. A lower WCSS value indicates that data points are closer to their respective cluster centers, meaning better clustering performance.

WCSS is commonly used in the Elbow Method to determine the optimal number of clusters (K). As the number of clusters increases, WCSS decreases, and the "elbow point" helps identify the most suitable value of K.

 

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{31}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{wcss}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output of WCSS is a list or array of values corresponding to different numbers of clusters (K values).

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{31}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
[269981.28,
 186362.95600651752,
 106348.37306211116,
 73880.64496247197,
 44454.47647967974,
 38797.9027638142,
 31600.209115340018,
 25056.895153616184,
 23311.584210674235,
 21797.817560054325,
 18092.765869660056]
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        

\subsection{Elbow Method Visualization for Optimal Clusters}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code is used to visualize the Elbow Method in K-Means clustering. The Elbow Method helps to determine the optimal number of clusters ($k$) by plotting the relationship between the number of clusters and the Within-Cluster Sum of Squares (WCSS).

Here:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{k\_range} represents different values of $k$ (number of clusters).
    \item \texttt{wcss} stores the sum of squared distances between data points and their respective cluster centroids.
    \item The graph shows how WCSS decreases as the number of clusters increases.
\end{itemize}

The point where the curve starts to bend (forming an "elbow") indicates the optimal number of clusters.

\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{32}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel} \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Number of Clusters(k)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel} \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sum of squared error}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{plot} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{k\PYZus{}range} \PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{wcss}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
A line plot is generated with:
\begin{itemize}
    \item X-axis: Number of Clusters ($k$)
    \item Y-axis: Sum of Squared Error (WCSS)
\end{itemize}

The graph typically shows a decreasing curve, and the "elbow point" is used to select the best value of $k$ for clustering.

\chapter{Naive Bayes Theorem}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-05: Implementing Naive Bayes Theorem}
\section*{Objective}
The objective of this experiment is to implement the Naive Bayes classification algorithm using the \texttt{CategoricalNB} model from the Scikit-learn library. The model is used to predict whether a customer will make a purchase based on categorical features such as Day, Discount availability, and Free Delivery option. Additionally, the experiment aims to understand the process of data preprocessing, label encoding, model training, and evaluation of classification performance.

\section*{Dataset Description}

The following datasets were used in this Naive Bayes lab experiment:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{NaiveBayesDataset.csv} \\
    This dataset is used for demonstrating the basic implementation of the Naive Bayes classification algorithm. It contains labeled features suitable for probability-based classification tasks.

    \item \textbf{titanic.csv} \\
    This dataset contains passenger information from the Titanic disaster. It is commonly used for classification problems such as predicting survival based on features like age, gender, and passenger class.

    \item \textbf{cardio\_train.csv} \\
    This dataset includes medical data used to predict cardiovascular disease. It contains features such as age, blood pressure, cholesterol level, and lifestyle indicators.

    \item \textbf{feature\_selection.csv} \\
    This dataset is used to analyze feature importance and selection techniques. It helps in identifying the most relevant attributes for improving classification performance.
\end{itemize}

Since all features are categorical in nature, label encoding is applied to convert them into numerical form before training the Naive Bayes classifier.    

    
\section{Implementing Naive Bayes
Theorem}\label{implementing-naive-bayes-theorem}

\subsection{Data Loading and Library Import for Naive Bayes}

\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, we import essential Python libraries required for building a Naive Bayes classification model. 
\texttt{pandas} is used for data handling and analysis. \texttt{train\_test\_split} helps to divide the dataset into training and testing sets. 
\texttt{CategoricalNB} is the Naive Bayes classifier suitable for categorical features. 
\texttt{LabelEncoder} is used to convert categorical variables into numerical form. 
Finally, \texttt{accuracy\_score} and \texttt{classification\_report} are used to evaluate model performance.

After importing the libraries, the dataset \texttt{NaiveBayesDataset.csv} is loaded using \texttt{pd.read\_csv()}, and displayed to verify its structure and contents.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{1}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pandas}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{naive\PYZus{}bayes}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{CategoricalNB}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{preprocessing}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{2}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{df}

21  Weekend      Yes           Yes       No
22  Holiday       No           Yes      Yes
23  Weekday      Yes           Yes      Yes
24  Holiday       No            No       No
25  Weekday       No           Yes       No
26  Weekday      Yes           Yes      Yes
27  Weekday      Yes           Yes      Yes
28  Holiday      Yes           Yes      Yes
29  Weekend      Yes           Yes      Yes
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        

\subsection{Encoding Categorical Variables using Label Encoding}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code converts categorical text data into numerical format using \texttt{LabelEncoder} from sklearn. A dictionary \texttt{label\_encoders} is created to store encoders for each column. The loop iterates through selected categorical columns: \texttt{Day}, \texttt{Discount}, \texttt{Free\_Delivery}, and \texttt{Purchase}. For each column, a new LabelEncoder object is initialized, and the categorical values are transformed into integer labels using \texttt{fit\_transform()}.
        

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encode categorical variables}
\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Day}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Discount}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Free\PYZus{}Delivery}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Purchase}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{le} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{le}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{le} 
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection{Train-Test Split and Categorical Naive Bayes Model Training}

\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, the dataset is divided into feature variables and target variable. The features \(X\) include \texttt{Day}, \texttt{Discount}, and \texttt{Free\_Delivery}, while the target variable \(y\) is \texttt{Purchase}, which indicates whether a customer made a purchase or not.

After defining the input and output variables, the dataset is split into training and testing sets using an 80:20 ratio. This allows the model to learn patterns from the training data and later be evaluated on unseen test data. The \texttt{random\_state=42} ensures reproducibility of the split.

Next, a \texttt{Categorical Naive Bayes (CategoricalNB)} model is initialized. This model is specifically suitable for categorical features. The model is then trained using the training dataset (\texttt{X\_train}, \texttt{y\_train}) to learn the probabilistic relationship between the features and the target variable.


        
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split data into features (X) and target (y)}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Day}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Discount}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Free\PYZus{}Delivery}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Purchase}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split into training and test sets}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Initialize and train the Naive Bayes model}
\PY{n}{nb\PYZus{}model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{CategoricalNB}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{nb\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

After executing this code, the model does not produce a direct printed output. However, internally:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The dataset is successfully split into training and testing sets.
    \item The Naive Bayes model is trained and fitted on the training data.
    \item The trained model is now ready to make predictions on the test set or new unseen data.
\end{itemize}
            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
CategoricalNB()
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection{Naive Bayes Model Prediction and Evaluation}
\subsection*{Explanation}

This code uses a trained Naive Bayes classifier (`nb\_model`) to make predictions on the test dataset (`X\_test`). The predicted class labels are stored in `y\_pred`.

After prediction, the model performance is evaluated using two key metrics:
- **Accuracy Score**: Measures the proportion of correctly classified samples in the test set.
- **Classification Report**: Provides detailed evaluation including precision, recall, F1-score, and support for each class.

These metrics help to understand how well the model generalizes to unseen data.

       
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Make predictions on the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{nb\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{9}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate the model}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Accuracy:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

The output displays:
- A single accuracy value (e.g., 0.85 or 85%), indicating overall model correctness.
- A classification report table showing precision, recall, and F1-score for each class.

From this output, we can interpret whether the model performs consistently across all classes or if it is biased toward any specific class. 

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Accuracy: 1.0
              precision    recall  f1-score   support

           1       1.00      1.00      1.00         6

    accuracy                           1.00         6
   macro avg       1.00      1.00      1.00         6
weighted avg       1.00      1.00      1.00         6

    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{Naive Bayes Prediction on New Example Data}

\subsection*{Explanation}

This code demonstrates how a trained Naive Bayes model is used to make a prediction on a new unseen sample. The input example consists of categorical features such as Day, Discount, and Free\_Delivery. Since machine learning models cannot directly process categorical text values, each feature is first converted into numerical form using previously fitted LabelEncoder objects.

After encoding, the processed data is passed into the trained Naive Bayes model (nb\_model) to predict whether a customer will make a purchase or not. The predicted numerical output is then converted back into its original categorical label (e.g., "Yes" or "No") using inverse\_transform() for better interpretability.

Finally, the code also prints the encoded classes for the Day feature to verify what categories the encoder has learned during training.

\textbf{Code Logic Summary:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Create a new input sample as a DataFrame
\item Encode categorical variables using label encoders
\item Predict output using trained Naive Bayes model
\item Convert prediction back to original label
\item Display encoded classes of a feature
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Output}

The output will display:
\begin{itemize}
\item The predicted purchase decision (e.g., "Purchase Prediction: Yes" or "No")
\item The list of encoded classes for the \textit{Day} feature (e.g., {Holiday, Weekend, Weekday})
\end{itemize}

This helps verify both the model’s prediction and the encoding structure used during preprocessing.
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{10}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example prediction}
\PY{n}{example} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Day}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Holiday}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Discount}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Yes}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Free\PYZus{}Delivery}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{No}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{example}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{example}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{example}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{prediction} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{nb\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{example}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Purchase Prediction:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Purchase}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.} \PY{n}{inverse\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{prediction}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Naive Bayes Classifier - Titanic}\label{naive-bayes-classifier---titanic}
\subsection{Loading Dataset using Pandas (head())}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this code, we import the Pandas library and load a CSV file named \texttt{titanic.csv} into a DataFrame using \texttt{pd.read\_csv()}. After loading the dataset, we use the \texttt{head()} function to display the first five rows of the dataset. This is commonly used to quickly inspect the structure, column names, and sample data of a dataset before performing further analysis or preprocessing.

\subsection{Handling Missing Values in Age Column}
\subsection*{Explanation}

This code handles missing (null) values in the \texttt{Age} column of the dataset using mean imputation. First, the mean value of the \texttt{Age} column is calculated and stored in the variable \texttt{handle}. Then, all null values in \texttt{Age} are replaced with this mean value using the \texttt{fillna()} function. This is a common preprocessing technique to preserve dataset size while maintaining statistical consistency.

       
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{23}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} removing null values of Age// better if we remove later on the updated dataset}
\PY{n}{handle} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{handle}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Output}

The output of this operation is the computed mean of the \texttt{Age} column (stored in \texttt{handle}). After execution, the \texttt{Age} column in the dataframe will no longer contain any missing values, as all NaNs are replaced with the calculated mean value.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Dropping Irrelevant Columns from Dataset}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, unnecessary features are removed from the dataset to simplify the model and reduce noise. The selected columns such as \texttt{PassengerId}, \texttt{Name}, \texttt{SibSp}, \texttt{Parch}, \texttt{Ticket}, \texttt{Cabin}, and \texttt{Embarked} are dropped because they are assumed to have weak or no direct influence on the target variable under the naive independence assumption. This helps improve model efficiency and focuses learning on more relevant features.
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{26}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Assumption: Make a naive assumption that features such as male,class,age,cabin,fare, etc. are independant of each other}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{27}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PassengerId}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Name}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{SibSp}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Parch}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Ticket}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Cabin}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Embarked}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{axis} \PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{columns}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{inplace}\PY{o}{=} \PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{28}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
