After executing the code, the specified columns are permanently removed from the dataframe using \texttt{inplace=True}. The command \texttt{df.head()} then displays the first five rows of the updated dataset, showing only the remaining relevant features such as survival-related attributes (e.g., class, sex, age, fare). The output confirms that the dataframe structure has been successfully reduced and cleaned for further analysis or model training.

\subsection{Data Preparation and Encoding}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, the dataset is prepared for model training. The \texttt{Survived} column is separated as the target variable, where 1 indicates survived and 0 indicates not survived. The remaining columns are treated as input features by dropping the \texttt{Survived} column.

Next, the categorical column \texttt{Sex} is converted into numerical form using one-hot encoding with \texttt{pd.get\_dummies()}. This creates separate columns (e.g., male and female) with binary values. The values are then converted from boolean (True/False) to integer (1/0) for better compatibility with machine learning models.
       
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{29}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 1 \PYZhy{} survived 0 \PYZhy{} not survived , At the end we will work on final}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} obtained from inputs and targer for train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split()}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{30}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} 1 \PYZhy{} survived 0 \PYZhy{} not survived }
\PY{n}{inputs}\PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Survived}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{axis} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{columns}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{target} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Survived}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{31}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encoding Sex}
\PY{n}{dummies} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get\PYZus{}dummies}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Sex}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{32}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{dummies} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{dummies}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} will display o and 1 instead of True and False}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{33}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{dummies}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

The output consists of a new dataframe containing encoded columns for the \texttt{Sex} feature. Each row will have values 0 or 1 indicating the presence of a category (male or female), instead of True/False.

\subsection{Merging DataFrames using pd.concat}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code combines two DataFrames, \texttt{inputs} and \texttt{dummies}, column-wise using the \texttt{pd.concat()} function. The parameter \texttt{axis = 'columns'} ensures that the DataFrames are merged horizontally, meaning the columns from \texttt{dummies} are added alongside the columns of \texttt{inputs}. The result is stored in a new DataFrame called \texttt{merged}.

        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{41}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{merged} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{inputs}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{dummies}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{axis} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{columns}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{42}{\boxspacing}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

The output displays a new DataFrame where all original columns from \texttt{inputs} and the encoded (dummy) columns from \texttt{dummies} are combined side by side. Each row corresponds correctly based on the index, resulting in an expanded dataset ready for further analysis or modeling.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\subsection{Handling Missing Values in 'Fare' Column}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The first line checks for columns in the dataset \texttt{final} that contain missing (NaN) values using \texttt{isna()} and returns only those column names. Here, it shows that the \texttt{'Fare'} column has missing values.

Next, the code calculates the total number of null values in the \texttt{'Fare'} column of the dataframe \texttt{df} using \texttt{isnull().sum()}. Finally, it prints the count in a formatted string.

\prompt{In}{incolor}{45}{\boxspacing}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

        \subsection{Handling Missing Fare Values and Displaying Data}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The statement \texttt{final.Fare = final.Fare.fillna(final.Fare.mean())} replaces all missing (NaN) values in the \texttt{Fare} column with the mean (average) fare value of that column. This is a common data preprocessing technique to handle missing data without removing rows.

The function \texttt{final.head()} is then used to display the first five rows of the dataset, allowing a quick check to confirm that missing values have been handled properly.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{48}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{final}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Fare} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{final}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Fare}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{final}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Fare}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{final}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{head}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the first five rows of the updated dataset. The \texttt{Fare} column no longer contains missing values, as they have been replaced by the average fare. This helps verify that the data cleaning step was successful.

\subsection{Naive Bayes Model Training and Testing}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The \texttt{train\_test\_split} function from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection} is used to divide the dataset into training and testing sets, where 70\% of the data is used for training and 30\% for testing. The \texttt{random\_state=1} ensures reproducibility of the split.

Then, a \texttt{GaussianNB} model (Naive Bayes classifier) is created and trained using \texttt{model.fit(xtrain, ytrain)}. After training, the model's performance is evaluated on the test data using \texttt{model.score(xtest, ytest)}, which returns the accuracy.

Finally, \texttt{xtest[:10]} is used to display the first 10 samples from the test dataset for inspection.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

 \subsection{Model Prediction and Probability Output}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The function \texttt{model.predict(xtest[0:10])} is used to predict the class labels (e.g., Survived or Not Survived) for the first 10 samples of the test dataset. The function \texttt{model.predict\_proba(xtest[:10])} returns the probability of each class for those same samples, showing how confident the model is in its predictions.

A custom test input \texttt{test = [[1,23.000000,113.2750,0]]} is provided, representing features like passenger class, age, fare, and gender. The model predicts the outcome for this input using \texttt{model.predict(test)}. Based on the predicted value, a conditional statement prints whether the passenger "Survived" or "Not Survived".

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

For the custom test input, the model returns a single prediction (0 or 1). If the result is 0, the output displayed is "Not Survived"; otherwise, it prints "Survived".

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{Cross Validation using Gaussian Naive Bayes}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The function \texttt{cross\_val\_score()} from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection} is used to evaluate the performance of the \texttt{GaussianNB()} model using cross-validation. Here, the dataset is split into 5 folds (cv = 5), where the model is trained on 4 folds and tested on the remaining fold. This process repeats 5 times, ensuring each fold is used once as test data. It helps in obtaining a more reliable estimate of model performance.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{59}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{60}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{GaussianNB}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,}\PY{n}{cv} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output is an array of 5 accuracy scores, where each value represents the model's performance on one fold. These scores indicate how well the model generalizes across different subsets of the training data.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

Then, it loads the dataset \texttt{cardio\_train.csv} using \texttt{pd.read\_csv()} with a semicolon (\texttt{;}) as the separator. The dataset is stored in a DataFrame named \texttt{df}. Finally, \texttt{df} is displayed to preview the dataset structure before applying the SelectKBest feature selection method.

\subsection{Dataset Shape and Class Distribution Analysis}
\subsection*{Explanation}
The expression \texttt{df.shape} is used to determine the dimensions of the dataset \texttt{df}. It returns a tuple representing the number of rows and columns in the dataset. In this case, the dataset contains 70,000 rows and 13 columns.

The function \texttt{df["cardio"].value\_counts()} is used to count the occurrences of each unique value in the \texttt{cardio} column. This is typically used to analyze the distribution of target classes in classification problems, helping to identify whether the dataset is balanced or imbalanced.

    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{Lab-05/output_70_1.png}

    \begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  x = df.iloc{[}:, :-1{]}:
\item
  This line is selecting all rows (:) and all columns except the last
  one (:-1).
\item
  : means ``select all rows.''
\item
  :-1 means ``select all columns except the last one.'' In Python
  slicing, :-1 excludes the last item, so this will include all columns
  up to, but not ncluding, the last one.
\item
  Result: x will be a DataFrame containing all the columns of df except
  the last column
  
\item
  x = df.iloc[: , :-1]
\item
  y = df.iloc{[}:, 12{]}
\item
  This line is selecting all rows (:) and the 13th column (remember,
  indexing in Python starts at 0, so index 12 corresponds to the 13th
  column).
\item
  : means ``select all rows.''
\item
  12 means ``select the 13th column'' (as Python uses 0-based indexing).
\item
  Result: y will be a Series containing the values of the 13th column of
  df.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Feature Selection using SelectKBest (ANOVA F-test)}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code performs feature selection on the dataset using \texttt{SelectKBest} with the \texttt{f\_classif} scoring function (ANOVA F-test). First, the dataset is split into features (\texttt{x}) and target (\texttt{y}) using \texttt{iloc}. Then, \texttt{SelectKBest} is initialized to evaluate the relationship between each feature and the target variable. The model is fitted using \texttt{fit(x, y)}, which computes statistical scores for each feature based on their importance. Finally, \texttt{FIT\_FEATURES.scores\_} is converted into a DataFrame to display feature importance scores in a structured format.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Detecting and Handling Non-Numeric Values in Dataset Columns}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code iterates through all columns of the dataframe \texttt{df} and checks their data types. If a column is of type \texttt{object} (usually containing strings or mixed values), it prints all unique values to identify non-numeric entries. After inspection, the entire dataframe is converted into numeric format using \texttt{pd.to\_numeric()} with \texttt{errors='coerce'}, which replaces invalid or non-convertible values with \texttt{NaN}. This is commonly used during preprocessing to clean data for machine learning models.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{86}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Identify non\PYZhy{}numeric values in specific columns}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{dtype} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{object}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Non\PYZhy{}numeric values in }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{:}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{unique}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{87}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Option 1: Convert columns to numeric, forcing errors to NaN}

Finally, the updated column names are printed again.

Finally, the updated dataframe is displayed with the encoded column replacing the original categorical values.

\subsection{Ordinal Encoding of Categorical Column}
\subsection*{Explanation}

First, an encoder object is created using \texttt{OrdinalEncoder()}. Then, the categorical column in the dataframe \texttt{df} is transformed using \texttt{fit\_transform()}, which assigns each unique category a corresponding integer value. This process is commonly used to prepare categorical data for machine learning models that require numerical input.

\end{Verbatim}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

The output is the updated dataframe \texttt{df}, where the specified categorical column is replaced with encoded numerical values. Each category is now represented by an integer, making the dataset suitable for further processing or model training.

\subsection{Encoding Multiple Categorical Columns with Validation}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code is used to handle encoding preparation for multiple categorical columns in a dataset. A list named \texttt{categorical\_columns} contains the column names \texttt{'owner'} and \texttt{'blodd group'}. The program iterates through each column and checks whether it exists in the DataFrame \texttt{df}. If a column is missing, it prints an error message indicating that the column does not exist. Otherwise, it confirms that the column is ready for encoding by printing a message.

This step is important in data preprocessing because encoding can only be applied to valid categorical columns. The check prevents runtime errors and ensures data integrity before transformation.

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{not} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
         \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Column }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ does not exist in the DataFrame}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
         \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Encoding column: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{column}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

This helps verify which categorical variables are available for encoding before further preprocessing.


\subsection{Encoding Multiple Categorical Columns Using OrdinalEncoder}
\subsection*{Explanation}
This code applies label encoding to multiple categorical columns in a dataset using \texttt{OrdinalEncoder}. First, it creates an encoder object. Then it filters only those columns from \texttt{categorical\_columns} that actually exist in the dataframe \texttt{df}. After that, it transforms all selected categorical columns into numerical values at once using \texttt{fit\_transform()}. This is useful for machine learning models that require numerical input instead of categorical text data.



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{117}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{encoder} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{OrdinalEncoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{existing\PYZus{}categorical\PYZus{}columns} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{col} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{categorical\PYZus{}columns} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{col} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{existing\PYZus{}categorical\PYZus{}columns}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{encoder}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{existing\PYZus{}categorical\PYZus{}columns}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

\chapter{Logistic Regression and SVM}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-06: Logistic Regression}

\section*{Objective}
The objective of this lab is to implement and analyze two supervised machine learning algorithms: Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machine (SVM). The goal is to understand how these classification techniques work on real-world datasets and to evaluate their performance in predicting categorical outcomes. 

Specifically, this lab aims to:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Apply Logistic Regression to model the probability of a binary outcome based on input features.
    \item Implement Support Vector Machine (SVM) to classify data by finding the optimal hyperplane.
    \item Compare the effectiveness of both models in terms of accuracy and decision boundaries.
    \item Gain practical experience in data preprocessing, model training, and evaluation.
\end{itemize}


Both models successfully classify the given datasets, allowing comparison between Logistic Regression and SVM in terms of prediction accuracy and performance.


\section*{Dataset Description}

In this lab, three datasets are used to demonstrate and evaluate the performance of Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machine (SVM):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{marital status.csv:} This dataset contains information related to individuals' marital status along with several independent features such as age, income, or other demographic attributes. It is used for general data exploration and preprocessing.

    \item \textbf{marital status\_Log\_Reg.csv:} This dataset is specifically prepared for Logistic Regression. It contains input features and a binary target variable representing marital status (e.g., Married or Unmarried), making it suitable for binary classification.

    \item \textbf{svm.csv:} This dataset is used for applying the Support Vector Machine algorithm. It typically includes feature variables and corresponding class labels, allowing SVM to determine the optimal separating hyperplane between classes.
\end{itemize}

These datasets provide a practical basis for understanding how different classification algorithms perform on structured data. The datasets are successfully loaded and utilized for training and testing both Logistic Regression and SVM models, enabling proper evaluation and comparison of results.
    
    

    
\section{Logistic Regression}\label{logistic-regression}

The \texttt{train\_test\_split} function from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection} is imported to divide the dataset into training and testing sets.

The dataset \texttt{marital status.csv} is then loaded into a DataFrame using \texttt{pandas.read\_csv()}, and the DataFrame is displayed to observe the structure and contents of the dataset.

\subsection{Handling Missing Values and Basic Statistics}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this code, missing values in the \texttt{age} column are handled by replacing them with the mean value of that column using the \texttt{fillna()} function. This ensures that the dataset remains consistent and no rows are dropped due to null values. 

The \texttt{info()} function is then used to display a summary of the dataset, including the number of non-null values and data types of each column. 

Finally, the median of the \texttt{marital\_status} column is calculated and stored in a variable, which can be useful for understanding the central tendency of the target variable.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

\PY{n}{updated\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{updated\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fillna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{updated\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{age}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{updated\PYZus{}df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{info}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

The dataset summary confirms the updated non-null counts and data types. Additionally, the computed median value of the \texttt{marital\_status} column is displayed as a numerical result.

\subsection{Handling Missing Values in Dataset}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, missing values in the \texttt{marital\_status} column are handled by replacing them with the median value stored in the variable \texttt{handle}. This ensures that the dataset does not contain null values, which could negatively affect model performance. 

After filling the missing values, the dataset is displayed to verify the changes. Then, the \texttt{isnull().sum()} function is used to confirm that there are no remaining null values in any column. Finally, \texttt{value\_counts()} is applied to the \texttt{marital\_status} column to observe the distribution of different classes.

\subsection{Dataset Splitting using train\_test\_split}
\subsection*{Explanation}

This code splits the dataset into training and testing subsets using the \texttt{train\_test\_split} function from \texttt{sklearn.model\_selection}. The input features (\texttt{x}) and target variable (\texttt{y}) are divided such that 70\% of the data is used for training and 30\% is used for testing (\texttt{test\_size = 0.30}). 

The parameter \texttt{random\_state = 1} ensures reproducibility, meaning the same split will be generated every time the code is executed. The resulting variables are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{xtrain}: Training feature set
    \item \texttt{xtest}: Testing feature set
    \item \texttt{ytrain}: Training labels
    \item \texttt{ytest}: Testing labels
\end{itemize}

Finally, \texttt{xtest} and \texttt{ytest} are displayed to observe the testing data samples and their corresponding labels.



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{19}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{20}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{}xtrain,ytrain,xtest,ytest = train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split(x,y,test\PYZus{}size= .20, random\PYZus{}state= 1)}
\PY{n}{xtrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{xtest}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytrain}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ytest} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{x}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.30}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state} \PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

The output shows a portion of the testing dataset:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{xtest}: Displays feature values of the test samples.
    \item \texttt{ytest}: Displays the actual class labels corresponding to those test samples.
\end{itemize}

This test data is later used to evaluate the performance of the trained machine learning model.

\subsection{Logistic Regression Model Initialization}
\subsection*{Explanation}

This code imports the Logistic Regression class from the \texttt{sklearn.linear\_model} module and initializes a Logistic Regression model. Logistic Regression is a supervised learning algorithm used for binary classification problems. The statement \texttt{model = LogisticRegression()} creates an instance of the model with default parameters, which can later be trained using training data. At this stage, no learning or fitting has occurred; the model is only prepared for training.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{23}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{linear\PYZus{}model}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{LogisticRegression}
\PY{n}{model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LogisticRegression} \PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}



\subsection{Model Training and Evaluation using Logistic Regression/SVM}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this step, the machine learning model is trained using the training dataset and then evaluated using the test dataset. The `fit()` function is used to train the model by learning patterns from the input features (`xtrain`) and corresponding labels (`ytrain`). After training, the `predict()` function is used to generate predictions on unseen test data (`xtest`). Finally, the `score()` function is used to measure the model’s performance by calculating its accuracy based on the comparison between predicted values and actual test labels (`ytest`).

\subsection*{Output}

The model is successfully trained on the training dataset and generates predictions for the test dataset. The final output shows the accuracy score of the model, which indicates how well the model performs in classifying unseen data. A higher score represents better model performance and better generalization capability.

\subsection{Model Training and Prediction using Logistic Regression}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this section, the Logistic Regression model is trained using the training dataset \texttt{xtrain} and \texttt{ytrain}. The \texttt{fit()} function is used to learn the relationship between input features and the target variable.

After training, the model is tested using \texttt{xtest} to generate predictions. The \texttt{predict()} function is used to classify the test data into discrete classes (e.g., Married or Not Married).

To evaluate the probability of each class, the \texttt{predict\_proba()} function is used. This function returns probability values for both classes, where the first value represents the probability of class ``No'' and the second value represents the probability of class ``Yes''.

Additionally, a custom prediction function is implemented to predict marital status based on a single input feature (age). The input age is reshaped into a 2D array and passed into the trained model. The output is then converted into a human-readable format.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{30}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predicting status of anyother single value providing age (one of the ways)}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Assuming you have a trained logistic regression model called \PYZsq{}model\PYZsq{}}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} model = LogisticRegression() \PYZsh{} Your trained model here}
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{predict\PYZus{}marriage\PYZus{}status}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{age}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Prepare the input as a 2D array}
    \PY{n}{age\PYZus{}input} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{array}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{age}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
    
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Get the prediction: 1 for married (Yes), 0 for not married (No)}
    \PY{n}{prediction} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{age\PYZus{}input}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}
    
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert prediction to readable format}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Yes}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{prediction} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{1} \PY{k}{else} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{No}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example usage}
\PY{n}{age} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{42} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Replace with any age you want to predict}
\PY{n}{status} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}marriage\PYZus{}status}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{age}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Prediction: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{status}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

The model successfully learns from the training data and predicts marital status on the test dataset. The \texttt{predict\_proba()} output shows probability distributions for each class, helping to understand model confidence.

For a sample input age (e.g., 42), the model returns a prediction such as:
\textbf{Prediction: Yes} or \textbf{Prediction: No}, indicating whether the person is likely married based on the trained logistic regression model.

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Prediction: Yes
    \end{Verbatim}



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{31}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predicting status of anyother single value providing age (second another way)}
\PY{n}{age} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{30} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Replace with any age you want to predict}
\PY{n}{status} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Yes}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{age}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+m+mi}{1} \PY{k}{else} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{NO}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Prediction: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{status}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{age} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{30} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Replace with any age you want to predict}
\PY{n}{status} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{No}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Yes}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{age}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Prediction: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{status}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\subsection{Data Loading}
\subsection*{Explanation}

First, all the necessary libraries are imported, including pandas for data handling, train\_test\_split for splitting the dataset, StandardScaler for feature scaling, LabelEncoder for encoding categorical variables, SVC for the SVM model, and evaluation metrics such as accuracy\_score and classification\_report.

After importing the libraries, the dataset \texttt{svm.csv} is loaded using \texttt{pandas.read\_csv()}. This dataset is then stored in a DataFrame named \texttt{data}, which is used for further preprocessing and model training.

\subsection{Data Preprocessing using Label Encoding for SVM}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this section, categorical variables in the dataset are converted into numerical format using \texttt{LabelEncoder}, which is necessary for applying the Support Vector Machine (SVM) algorithm.

The columns \texttt{Habitat} and \texttt{Species} contain categorical text values, which cannot be directly processed by the SVM model. Therefore, \texttt{LabelEncoder} is used to transform these categorical values into numeric labels.

First, the \texttt{Habitat} column is encoded into numerical values, and then the same encoder is used to convert the \texttt{Species} column into numeric form. This ensures that all input features are in a machine-readable format suitable for training the SVM classifier.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encode the categorical \PYZsq{}Habitat\PYZsq{} and \PYZsq{}Species\PYZsq{} columns}
\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoder} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Habitat}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoder}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Habitat}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)} 

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert habitat to numeric}
\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Species}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoder}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Species}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)} 

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert species to numeric}
\PY{n}{data} 
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

After applying label encoding, both \texttt{Habitat} and \texttt{Species} columns are successfully converted into numeric values. The dataset is now fully prepared for training the SVM model, enabling it to perform classification based on the transformed features.

\subsection{SVM Classifier Training and Evaluation}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this section, a Support Vector Machine (SVM) classifier is implemented to classify species based on physical and environmental features. First, the dataset is divided into input features \texttt{X} (Length, Weight, Snout Width, Habitat) and target variable \texttt{y} (Species).

The dataset is then split into training and testing sets using an 70-30 ratio to evaluate model performance on unseen data. To improve model efficiency and ensure all features contribute equally, feature scaling is applied using \texttt{StandardScaler}.

An SVM model with a linear kernel is initialized using \texttt{SVC(kernel='linear', C=1.0)}. The linear kernel is suitable for linearly separable data and helps in finding an optimal hyperplane that separates the classes.

After training the model using \texttt{fit()}, predictions are made on the test dataset using \texttt{predict()}. The model performance is evaluated using accuracy score and a classification report, which includes precision, recall, and F1-score for each class.
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Separate features and target}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Length}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Weight}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Snout Width}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Habitat}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Species}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split the data into training and testing sets}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} 
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.3}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Standardize the feature values}
\PY{n}{scaler} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{StandardScaler}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Initialize and train the SVM model}
\PY{n}{svm\PYZus{}model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{SVC}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{kernel}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{linear}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{C}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{1.0}\PY{p}{)} 
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Linear kernel SVM for binary classification}
\PY{n}{svm\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{SVC}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{kernel}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{linear}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
SVC(kernel='linear')
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{8}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Make predictions on the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{svm\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate the model}
\PY{n}{accuracy} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{report} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{,} 
\PY{n}{target\PYZus{}names}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Alligator}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Crocodile}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{9}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Output the results}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Accuracy:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{accuracy}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Classification Report:}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{report}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Habitat and Species Encoding using Mapping Dictionaries}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this section, categorical data is converted into numerical format using mapping dictionaries, which is a necessary preprocessing step for machine learning models like Support Vector Machine (SVM).

The \texttt{habitat\_mapping} dictionary is used to convert habitat types into numerical values, where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textit{Freshwater} is encoded as 0
    \item \textit{Saltwater} is encoded as 1
\end{itemize}

Similarly, the \texttt{species\_mapping} dictionary is used to convert predicted numeric class labels into meaningful species names:
\begin{itemize}
    \item 0 represents \textit{Alligator}
    \item 1 represents \textit{Crocodile}
\end{itemize}

This encoding helps the SVM model process categorical inputs efficiently and also improves interpretability of the output results.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{10}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define the habitat mapping outside the function}
\PY{n}{habitat\PYZus{}mapping} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
 \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Freshwater}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,}
 \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Saltwater}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{11}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define species mapping as well, for completeness}
\PY{n}{species\PYZus{}mapping} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Alligator}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Crocodile}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}



\subsection{SVM Classification and Species Prediction}
\subsection*{Explanation}

In this section, a Support Vector Machine (SVM) model is used to classify an input sample into one of two species: Crocodile or Alligator. A custom prediction function \texttt{predict\_species()} is defined to handle new input data.

First, the categorical feature \texttt{habitat} is converted into a numerical value using a predefined mapping (\texttt{habitat\_mapping}). If an invalid habitat is provided, the function displays an error message and stops execution.

Next, the input features (length, weight, snout width, and encoded habitat) are combined into a DataFrame to ensure consistency with the training dataset structure. This data is then scaled using the previously fitted scaler (\texttt{scaler}) to match the preprocessing applied during training.

The processed input is passed to the trained SVM model (\texttt{svm\_model}) to generate a prediction. The numerical output is then converted back into a meaningful class label (Crocodile or Alligator) using \texttt{species\_mapping}.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{12}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Function to predict whether a new input is a Crocodile or Alligator}
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{predict\PYZus{}species}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{length}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{weight}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{snout\PYZus{}width}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{habitat}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
     \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert habitat to numeric using the previously saved mapping}
     \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{habitat} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{habitat\PYZus{}mapping}\PY{p}{:}
      \PY{n}{habitat\PYZus{}num} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{habitat\PYZus{}mapping}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{habitat}\PY{p}{]}
     \PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
      \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Invalid habitat. Please use }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Freshwater}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ or }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Saltwater}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{.}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
      \PY{k}{return}
     \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Prepare the feature vector and scale it}
     \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert the new data into a DataFrame with the same columns as X\PYZus{}train}
     \PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{length}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{weight}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{snout\PYZus{}width}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{habitat\PYZus{}num}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
                             \PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Length}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Weight}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Snout Width}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Habitat}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
     \PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data\PYZus{}scaled} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{scaler}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{)}
     \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict using the SVM model}
     \PY{n}{prediction} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{svm\PYZus{}model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{new\PYZus{}data\PYZus{}scaled}\PY{p}{)}

     \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Map the numeric prediction back to species name}
     \PY{n}{species\PYZus{}name} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{species\PYZus{}mapping}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{prediction}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
     \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{The predicted species is: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{species\PYZus{}name}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example input to check the species}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example: Length=4.0 meters, Weight=500 kg, Snout Width=22 cm,}
\PY{n}{Habitat}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Freshwater}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}
\PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}species}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mf}{4.0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{500}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{22}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Freshwater}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}

The model successfully predicts the species based on the given input features. For example, for input values:

\texttt{Length = 4.0 meters, Weight = 500 kg, Snout Width = 22 cm, Habitat = Freshwater}

the model outputs:

\textbf{The predicted species is: Crocodile} (or Alligator depending on model decision boundary).

This demonstrates that the SVM model can effectively classify unseen data based on learned patterns from the training dataset.

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
The predicted species is: Alligator
    \end{Verbatim}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{13}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}species}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mf}{3.4}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{400}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{20}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Saltwater}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \end{Verbatim}

\chapter{Decision Tree and Confusion Matrix}
\rhead{Machine Learning Lab}
\lhead{Lab-07: Decision Tree and Confusion Matrix}

\section*{Objective}

The objective of this experiment is to implement the Decision Tree algorithm for classification tasks. This experiment aims to build a model that can classify data into distinct categories based on feature values by learning decision rules from the dataset. It also focuses on understanding how tree-based models split data and evaluate their classification performance.

The objective of this experiment is to apply the Decision Tree algorithm for regression tasks. The goal is to predict continuous target values by learning patterns from input features using a tree-structured model. This experiment helps in understanding how decision trees can approximate numerical relationships and handle non-linear data.

The objective of this experiment is to evaluate the performance of a classification model using a confusion matrix. It aims to analyze the model's prediction results by comparing actual and predicted class labels, and to compute performance metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score for better assessment of the model.


\section*{Dataset Description}

Three different datasets are used in this lab to demonstrate the application of regression algorithms.

\subsection*{Dataset Description: DT.csv}
The \texttt{DT.csv} dataset is used for implementing Decision Tree algorithms. It contains multiple attributes representing different features of the data along with a target variable used for classification or prediction. The dataset is structured to demonstrate how a Decision Tree model splits data based on feature values to make decisions. It is suitable for understanding tree-based learning, feature importance, and rule-based classification.

\subsection*{Dataset Description: house\_data.csv}
The \texttt{house\_data.csv} dataset contains real estate information used for predicting house prices. It includes several numerical and possibly categorical features such as area, number of bedrooms, location factors, and other property-related attributes. The target variable represents the house price. This dataset is used to analyze how different factors influence property prices and to apply regression techniques for accurate prediction.

\section{Decision Tree Classifier}\label{decision-tree-classifier}

\subsection{Implementing on Tennis Dataset}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code imports essential machine learning libraries such as pandas for data handling, scikit-learn modules for building and evaluating a Decision Tree classifier. The dataset is loaded from a CSV file named \texttt{DT.csv}, which likely contains tennis-related attributes used for classification. A Decision Tree model is typically used to learn decision rules from data features and predict categorical outcomes.

\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{preprocessing}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{ConfusionMatrixDisplay}

\subsection{Data Preprocessing and Model Training}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code performs preprocessing and model training for a Decision Tree classifier. First, categorical variables in the dataset are converted into numeric form using \texttt{LabelEncoder}, since machine learning models cannot directly process text data. Each column (except identifiers like \texttt{Day}) is encoded and stored.  

Next, the dataset is split into feature variables \texttt{X} (Outlook, Temperature, Humidity, Wind) and target variable \texttt{y} (PlayTennis). The data is then divided into training and testing sets using an 80-20 split.  

A \texttt{DecisionTreeClassifier} is trained using the training data, and predictions are made on the test set.

      
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{4}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encoding categorical variables}
\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{column} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Ignore \PYZsq{}Day\PYZsq{} as it’s an identifier}
    \PY{n}{le} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{LabelEncoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{le}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{column}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{le}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Separating features and target variable}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Outlook}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Temp}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Humidity}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Wind}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Feature columns}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PlayTennis}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Target column}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split the data into training and testing sets}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train the Decision Tree classifier}
\PY{n}{model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{DecisionTreeClassifier}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Make predictions on the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]

\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

The output includes the trained model and predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred}) for the test dataset. The comparison between \texttt{y\_pred} and actual values (\texttt{y\_test}) shows how well the model performs. If printed, both arrays will display encoded class labels (e.g., 0 and 1), which can later be evaluated using accuracy or confusion matrix.

\subsection{Decision Tree Model Accuracy Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code calculates the performance of the trained Decision Tree classifier by comparing the predicted labels (\texttt{y\_pred}) with the actual test labels (\texttt{y\_test}). The function \texttt{accuracy\_score} from scikit-learn is used to compute the proportion of correctly classified instances. The result is stored in the variable \texttt{accuracy} and then printed.

      
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{10}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Calculate and print accuracy}
\PY{n}{accuracy} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Model accuracy:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{accuracy}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays the model’s accuracy as a numeric value between 0 and 1 (or 0\% to 100\%). 
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Model accuracy: 1.0
    \end{Verbatim}

\subsection{Decision Tree - Confusion Matrix Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code evaluates the performance of the trained Decision Tree classifier using a confusion matrix. The function \texttt{confusion\_matrix(y\_test, y\_pred)} compares the actual labels (\texttt{y\_test}) with the predicted labels (\texttt{y\_pred}) and produces a matrix showing correct and incorrect classifications. Each row represents actual classes, while each column represents predicted classes. Additionally, \texttt{ConfusionMatrixDisplay.from\_predictions()} visualizes this matrix with proper class labels using the encoded target variable \texttt{PlayTennis}.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{11}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Display confusion matrix}
\PY{n}{conf\PYZus{}matrix} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Confusion Matrix:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{conf\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{ConfusionMatrixDisplay}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{from\PYZus{}predictions}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{display\PYZus{}labels}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PlayTennis}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{classes\PYZus{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output includes a printed confusion matrix in numerical form, showing how many predictions were correct and where misclassifications occurred. 
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Confusion Matrix:
[[1 0]
 [0 2]]
\end{Verbatim}

\subsection{Decision Tree - Actual vs Predicted Comparison}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code compares the actual target values with the predicted values generated by the Decision Tree classifier. It iterates through the test dataset using \texttt{y\_test} (true labels) and \texttt{y\_pred} (model predictions). Since the labels were originally encoded using \texttt{LabelEncoder}, the code converts them back to their original categorical form (e.g., Yes/No for PlayTennis) using \texttt{inverse\_transform} for better interpretability.



\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{12}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Show actual vs predicted values for each test sample}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Actual vs Predicted:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{actual}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{predicted} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{zip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Actual: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PlayTennis}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inverse\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{actual}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PlayTennis}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inverse\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{predicted}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output prints a list of test samples showing side-by-side comparison of actual vs predicted values. Each line displays the true class (e.g., PlayTennis = Yes/No) and the model’s predicted class for that same instance. This helps evaluate how accurately the Decision Tree classifier is performing on unseen data.


\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Actual vs Predicted:
Actual: Yes, Predicted: Yes
Actual: Yes, Predicted: Yes
Actual: No, Predicted: No
    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{Decision Tree Prediction on New Input}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code prepares a new input sample for prediction using a trained Decision Tree model. Since machine learning models require numerical input, categorical values such as \texttt{Outlook}, \texttt{Temp}, \texttt{Humidity}, and \texttt{Wind} are first converted into encoded numeric form using previously fitted \texttt{LabelEncoder} objects. The input corresponds to a condition: rain, mild temperature, high humidity, and strong wind. After preprocessing, the model predicts whether tennis should be played based on learned patterns from the dataset. Finally, the predicted numeric output is converted back into a readable label using inverse transformation.

\subsection*{Output}
The model outputs a single classification result indicating whether playing tennis is suitable under the given weather conditions. For the input \{rain, mild, high, strong\}, the printed output will typically show either \textbf{Play Tennis = Yes} or \textbf{Play Tennis = No}, depending on the patterns learned during training. This confirms that the model can generalize and make decisions on unseen data.
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{13}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example prediction for new input \PYZob{}rain, mild, high, strong\PYZcb{}}
\PY{n}{input\PYZus{}data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Outlook}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Outlook}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{rain}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Temp}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Temp}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{mild}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Humidity}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Humidity}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{high}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Wind}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Wind}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{strong}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{14}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Predict whether to play tennis}
\PY{n}{prediction} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{input\PYZus{}data}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{result} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{label\PYZus{}encoders}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{PlayTennis}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inverse\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{prediction}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Prediction for input }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{rain, mild, high, strong\PYZcb{}: Play Tennis =}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{result}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

Prediction for input \{rain, mild, high, strong\}: Play Tennis = No
    \end{Verbatim}

\section{Decision tree from Dataset to predict as a
Regressor}\label{decision-tree-from-dataset-to-predict-as-a-regressor}

\subsection{Decision Tree Regressor - House Price Prediction}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code implements a Decision Tree Regressor model to predict house prices using a dataset named \texttt{house\_data\_DT.csv}. First, necessary libraries are imported, including pandas for data handling, train\_test\_split for splitting the dataset, and DecisionTreeRegressor for regression modeling. The dataset is then loaded and cleaned by stripping whitespace from column names and validating the presence of the \texttt{Location} column.

The \texttt{Location} feature is converted into numerical form using manual encoding (Suburb = 0, City = 1). Missing or invalid values are checked, and any incomplete rows are removed to ensure data consistency. After preprocessing, the dataset is split into features (Rooms, Area, Location) and target variable (Price). Finally, the data is divided into training and testing sets, and a Decision Tree Regressor is trained using the training data.



    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{15}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pandas}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{tree}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{DecisionTreeRegressor}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{mean\PYZus{}squared\PYZus{}error}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{r2\PYZus{}score}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{16}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load the dataset}
\PY{n}{data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{read\PYZus{}csv}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{house\PYZus{}data\PYZus{}DT.csv}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{17}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
 \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Ensure all column names are stripped of whitespace}
\PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{str}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{strip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Check if \PYZsq{}Location\PYZsq{} exists}
\PY{k}{if} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{o+ow}{not} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{raise} \PY{n+ne}{KeyError}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Location}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ column is missing!}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert all values in the \PYZsq{}Location\PYZsq{} column to strings and strip whitespace}
\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{str}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{str}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{strip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encode the \PYZsq{}Location\PYZsq{} column}
\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{map}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Suburb}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{City}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Check for missing or unmapped values}
\PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isnull}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{any}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Warning: Some rows in }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Location}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ have unmapped or invalid values!}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{isnull}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{dropna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Drop rows with unmapped values if necessary}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{18}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define features (X) and target (y)}
\PY{n}{X} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rooms}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Area}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Price}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split the data into training and testing sets}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{19}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train the Decision Tree Regressor}
\PY{n}{regressor} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{DecisionTreeRegressor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
\subsection*{Output}
The output of this stage includes a cleaned and preprocessed dataset with no missing or invalid values, followed by successful training of the Decision Tree Regressor model. No prediction is shown yet at this step, but the model is now ready to estimate house prices based on input features in later stages.
            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{19}{\boxspacing}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        


\subsection{Decision Tree Prediction and Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code uses a trained Decision Tree regressor model to make predictions on the test dataset (\texttt{X\_test}). The predicted values (\texttt{y\_pred}) are then compared with the actual target values (\texttt{y\_test}) to evaluate model performance. Two evaluation metrics are used: Mean Squared Error (MSE), which measures the average squared difference between actual and predicted values, and R² Score, which indicates how well the model explains the variance in the data.



\begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{20}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Make predictions on the test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate the model}
\PY{n}{mse} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{mean\PYZus{}squared\PYZus{}error}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{r2} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{r2\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mean Squared Error: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{mse}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{R\PYZca{}2 Score: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{r2}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Output}
The output displays two numerical evaluation results. The Mean Squared Error (MSE) shows how much error the model is making on average (lower is better). The R² Score indicates the goodness of fit, where a value closer to 1 means the model is performing well in predicting the target variable. These results help assess the accuracy and reliability of the Decision Tree model.

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Mean Squared Error: 2500000000.0
R\^{}2 Score: -10.11111111111111
    \end{Verbatim}


\subsection{Decision Tree Price Prediction Function}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code defines a function \texttt{predict\_price()} that takes user inputs such as number of rooms, area, and location to predict a house price using a trained regression model (referred to as \texttt{regressor}). The categorical variable \texttt{location} is manually encoded into numerical form (Suburb = 0, City = 1) to match the model’s training format. The inputs are then converted into a pandas DataFrame and passed into the model’s \texttt{predict()} function to generate the estimated price.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{21}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Function to predict price based on user input}
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{predict\PYZus{}price}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rooms}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{area}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{location}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Encode the location input to match training data encoding}
    \PY{n}{location\PYZus{}encoded} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{0} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{location}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{lower}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{suburb}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{k}{else} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}
    
    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Prepare the input data}
    \PY{n}{input\PYZus{}data} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Rooms}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{rooms}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Area}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{area}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Location}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{location\PYZus{}encoded}\PY{p}{]}
    \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Make the prediction}
    \PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}price} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{regressor}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{input\PYZus{}data}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted Price for input (Rooms: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, Area: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, Location: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{): \PYZdl{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}:,.2f\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{format}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rooms}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{area}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{location}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{predicted\PYZus{}price}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Example prediction}
\PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}price}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rooms}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{3}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{area}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{75}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{location}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Suburb}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{predict\PYZus{}price}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rooms}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{4}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{area}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{95}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{location}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{City}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


\subsection*{Output}
The function prints the predicted house price for given input values. For example, when called with (Rooms=3, Area=75, Location='Suburb'), it outputs a lower estimated price compared to (Rooms=4, Area=95, Location='City'), as larger area and city location typically increase property value. The output is displayed in a formatted currency style showing the predicted price for each test case.

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

Predicted Price for input (Rooms: 3, Area: 75, Location: Suburb): \$320,000.00

Predicted Price for input (Rooms: 4, Area: 95, Location: City): \$450,000.00
    \end{Verbatim}
