\section{Confusion Matrix}\label{confusion-matrix}
\lhead{Lab-07: Confusion Matrix}

\subsection{Confusion Matrix for Classification Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code demonstrates how to evaluate a classification model using a confusion matrix. It imports necessary libraries including pandas, matplotlib, seaborn, and scikit-learn metrics. A custom function \texttt{plot\_confusion\_matrix()} is defined to visualize the confusion matrix using a heatmap. The actual labels (\texttt{truth}) and predicted labels (\texttt{prediction}) are defined for a binary classification problem (Dog vs Not a dog). The confusion matrix is then computed using \texttt{confusion\_matrix()} from scikit-learn, which compares actual vs predicted values and summarizes correct and incorrect classifications.


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{22}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pandas}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{plt}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{seaborn}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sns}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix} \PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{23}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{plot\PYZus{}confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{conf\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{class\PYZus{}names}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}cm} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{conf\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{class\PYZus{}names}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{class\PYZus{}names}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{sns}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{heatmap}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}cm}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{annot}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{fmt}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{d}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cmap}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Blues}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Actual}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Predicted}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}


    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{24}{\boxspacing}

\subsection*{Output}
The output is a 2×2 confusion matrix heatmap showing the classification performance. The diagonal values represent correctly classified samples (true positives and true negatives), while the off-diagonal values represent misclassifications. The heatmap visually highlights how many "Dog" and "Not a dog" instances were correctly or incorrectly predicted by the model, making it easier to evaluate accuracy and error patterns.

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{Lab-07/output_29_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}


\subsection{Confusion Matrix and Classification Report Evaluation}

\subsection*{Explanation}
This code evaluates a classification model using the \texttt{classification\_report} function, which provides precision, recall, and F1-score for each class by comparing the true labels (\texttt{truth}) with the predicted labels (\texttt{prediction}). 

Additionally, the F1-score is manually computed for two classes: “Dog” and “not a Dog” using the harmonic mean formula:
\[
F1 = 2 \times \frac{Precision \times Recall}{Precision + Recall}
\]
For the “Dog” class, precision is 0.57 and recall is 0.67. For the “not a Dog” class, precision is 0.33 and recall is 0.25. These calculations help measure the balance between precision and recall for each class.

\subsection*{Output}
The output includes a full classification report showing precision, recall, F1-score, and support for each class. It indicates how well the model distinguishes between “Dog” and “not a Dog” instances. The manually computed F1-scores further confirm model performance: the “Dog” class achieves a moderate F1-score (~0.61), while the “not a Dog” class shows a low F1-score (~0.29), indicating weaker predictive performance for that class.

%================ CONCLUSION =================
\chapter*{\center Conclusion}

This lab report successfully demonstrates the practical implementation of fundamental machine learning techniques across multiple experiments. Each lab contributed to building a comprehensive understanding of data handling, model development, and evaluation.

The experiments highlighted the importance of preprocessing in improving data quality and model accuracy. Regression techniques provided insights into predictive modeling, while classification algorithms such as KNN, Naive Bayes, Logistic Regression, SVM, and Decision Trees showcased different approaches to solving classification problems.

Furthermore, encoding techniques enabled effective handling of categorical variables, and evaluation tools like the confusion matrix helped assess model performance in a structured manner.

Overall, the lab sessions provided valuable hands-on experience and reinforced theoretical concepts, preparing a strong foundation for advanced machine learning applications and real-world problem solving.
